exchangeability, the observed WHO ratio is typical of nulls* — the
helpers carry no structure beyond what random within-row rearrangement
would produce. The null *median* ratio under within-row shuffle is
27.23×, **higher** than observed (22.11×). Under this null, with five
rows of substantially different row-means (row spread 34.0 pp), even
a random rearrangement of helper labels within each row produces col
means with high apparent spread when the rows are sampled
unevenly — so the ratio under shuffle is wide and right-skewed.

The within-column p-value of **<0.0001** says: *under within-helper
primary exchangeability, the observed ratio is far higher than the
null distribution* — the primary effect is highly significant.

The §6t strict-shuffle p = 0.0010 is the *combined* test. The
decomposition shows it is *entirely* driven by the primary effect:
the helper component contributes p = 0.65, no signal.

### Triangulation with §6r ANOVA

The four statistical tests now in the audit converge on the same
qualitative answer:

| Test | Primary effect | Helper effect | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| §6f within-cell bootstrap | 95% CI [1.89, 8.39] (excludes 1) | (combined ratio) | §6f |
| §6f question-clustered bootstrap | 95% CI [2.20, 7.45] (excludes 1) | (combined ratio) | §6f revised |
| §6r replicate-aware ANOVA | F(4,1470) = 26.55, p < 1e-10 | F(5,1470) = 0.96, p = 0.44 | §6r |
| §6t strict-shuffle permutation | (combined p = 0.001) | (combined p = 0.001) | §6t |
| §6u within-column permutation | p < 0.0001 | — | §6u this section |
| §6u within-row permutation | — | p = 0.65 | §6u this section |

**Primary effect**: 4/4 tests reject H0 (no primary effect).
**Helper effect**: 2/2 tests fail to reject H0 (no helper effect).

The §6u within-row p-value (0.65) is the cleanest "helper effect is
absent" statement in the audit. It is robust to any normal-error
assumption (unlike F-tests) and respects the question-clustering
structure (unlike strict shuffle).

### Implication for the queue's hypothesis

The original audit follow-up #15 hypothesis was: *"clustered
permutation gives a slightly larger p-value but still well under
0.05."* This was implicitly framing the test as a WHO-ratio test
(primary + helper jointly) and predicted a small but positive
p-value.

**Hypothesis falsified for the within-row formulation.** When you
decompose into helper-only (within-row, p = 0.65) and primary-only
(within-column, p < 0.0001), the helper-only test does *not* show
"slightly larger p-value still under 0.05" — it gives p = 0.65,
clearly not significant. The within-column test gives p < 0.0001,
*smaller* than the strict-shuffle p = 0.001 because primary effect
alone is more concentrated than the combined test.

The audit follow-up #15 expectation was wrong because the queue
description conflated "the WHO ratio test" with "the primary effect
test." Under cluster-preserving permutation, the WHO ratio test
splits cleanly into a strong primary effect (p < 1e-4) and a null
helper effect (p = 0.65). Both are correct readings of the same
data; the strict-shuffle p = 0.001 sits in between.

### Implication for the paper

The §10 abstract directive's *third* revision should add (or
replace existing helper-side language):

> *Under cluster-respecting permutation (within-row shuffle of
> helpers preserving the primary's question pool), the observed
> WHO ratio is typical of the null distribution (p = 0.65, n = 5000
> iterations) — helpers are exchangeable within primaries.
> Under within-column shuffle, the observed ratio is far above the
> null (p < 0.0001) — primaries are not exchangeable within
> helpers. The combined strict-shuffle p = 0.001 (audit §6t) is the
> conservative test; the decomposition (audit §6u) attributes
> 100% of the rejection to the primary effect.*

This is the strongest non-parametric statement the paper can make
about the WHO-asymmetry. It complements §6r's parametric ANOVA
(F = 26.55, p < 1e-10 primary; F = 0.96, p = 0.44 helper) — both
the parametric and non-parametric tests reach exactly the same
conclusion.

## 6v. Per-question helper-correctness vs helper self-solo — no signal

The §6s finding was that 49.2% of questions have helpers disagree on
correctness — there is per-question helper-induced variation that
the cell-mean §6r ANOVA could not detect. A natural follow-up: *does
the disagreement correlate with helper competence?* If so, a "good
helper" model would pick the strongest-self-solo helper for each
question; if not, helper-induced variation is noise.

Audit follow-up #16 (script:
`src/scripts/per_q_helper_correlation.py`, output:
`results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/per_q_helper_correlation.json`):
for each `(primary, idx)` tuple, compute Spearman ρ between the 6
helpers' correctness vector (boolean) and the 6 helpers' self-solo
accuracy vector (the helper specialist's accuracy on its OWN domain;
base helper's self-solo = mean(base_solo across primaries) = 0.248).

| Primary | All-helpers-correct | All-helpers-wrong | Disagree | Mean ρ on disagree | Mean ρ on hard (1–3 correct) | Median ρ on disagree |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| math | 7 | 17 | 26 | −0.044 | +0.026 | +0.000 |
| medicine | 10 | 12 | 28 | +0.099 | −0.004 | +0.049 |
| biology | 24 | 6 | 20 | +0.086 | +0.153 | +0.131 |
| law | 6 | 19 | 25 | −0.081 | −0.104 | +0.000 |
| physics | 11 | 15 | 24 | **+0.271** | +0.093 | **+0.393** |
| **pooled** | **58** | **69** | **123** | **+0.064** | **+0.009** | **+0.000** |

(All 250 (primary, idx) records: 58 unanimous-correct, 69
unanimous-wrong, 123 disagree.)

**P(ρ > 0 \| disagree) pooled = 49.6%** — indistinguishable from
chance (50% under H0 of no effect).

### Reading

- **Pooled correlation is essentially zero.** Helper self-solo does
  not predict which helper gets a disagree-question correct on the
  primary's domain. The hypothesis "hard questions are solved by
  helpers with strong primary-domain competence" is **falsified**
  at the pooled level on this dataset.
- **Physics is the only primary with a moderately positive ρ
  (+0.27).** This is interpretable: physics primary is the weakest
  specialist (self-solo 12%; §1) so its row is dominated by very
  hard questions. On hard questions, the strongest-self-solo helper
  has *some* relative advantage, but the effect is moderate and not
  reproduced in other primaries. Physics' median ρ on disagree
  questions is +0.39 — the largest in the table by 2×.
- **Math and law are flat.** Math's ρ ≈ 0; law's ρ ≈ −0.08, both
  with median 0.000. Helper self-solo carries no information about
  per-question outcome for these primaries.
- **Biology and medicine show small positive ρ (~+0.09).** Marginal,
  consistent with the §6h "helpers with stronger self-solo are
  slightly better helpers on average" observation but at the
  per-question level the effect is much weaker than it looks at
  the col-mean level.

### Reconciliation with §6h

§6h reported Pearson r(self_solo, col_mean_delta) = **+0.51**
across the 5 specialists, suggesting "better specialists make better
helpers" at the col-mean level. §6v finds *no consistent per-question
signal* in the same direction: the cell-mean correlation comes from
averaging over questions, not from a per-question relationship
between helper self-solo and outcome. **§6h's correlation is a
between-helper aggregation, not a within-question prediction.**

This refines the §6h interpretation:

> *The col-mean Pearson r(self_solo, col_mean_delta) = +0.51 (n=5)
> reflects average helper quality differences. At the per-question
> level, the analogous Spearman ρ pooled across all disagree
> questions is +0.064 (essentially zero), and only one of five
> primaries (physics) shows a positive per-question correlation.
> Helper self-solo predicts col-mean outcome moderately but does
> not predict question-level outcome.*

### Final triangulation summary

The audit now contains five tests targeting "does helper identity
matter?":

| Test | Level | Effect direction | p-value or signal |
|---|---|---|---|
| §6r ANOVA helper main effect | cell mean | none | F = 0.96, p = 0.44 |
| §6t strict-shuffle (combined) | cell value | combined primary + helper | p = 0.001 |
| §6u within-row permutation | cell mean | helper-only | p = 0.65 |
| §6h col-mean vs self-solo | helper aggregate | weak positive | r = +0.51 (n=5) |
| §6v per-q vs self-solo | per question | none | ρ = +0.064 (P(ρ>0) = 50%) |

§6v is the highest-resolution helper test. It says: at the level
where the §6s analysis found 49.2% of questions show helper-induced
disagreement, the disagreement is **not predictable from helper
competence**. The §6r and §6u tests had already shown that the
helper main effect averages out at the cell-mean level; §6v shows
that even at the per-question level, helper identity does not carry
*structure* — it carries *noise*.

This is a stronger negative result for the helper main effect than
the audit had so far: not only is it small in aggregate, it is
also unstructured per-question. The combined picture:

- Primary effect is a strong, well-resolved signal at every level
  (cell mean F = 26.55, ANOVA p < 1e-10; permutation p < 0.0001
  within-column; bootstrap CI [2.20, 7.45]; subject-stratified
  43.4%-of-total-variance).
- Helper effect is zero at the cell-mean level (ANOVA p = 0.44,
  permutation p = 0.65) AND at the per-question level (ρ = +0.064
  pooled, P(ρ>0) = 50%).

For the paper:

> *Across 123 questions (of 250 (primary, idx) records) where the
> 6 helpers disagree on correctness, the per-question Spearman ρ
> between helper self-solo accuracy and helper correctness is +0.064
> (pooled, P(ρ > 0) = 49.6%). The helper effect that surfaces at the
> per-question level (audit §6s: 49.2% of questions show
> helper-induced disagreement) is unstructured by helper competence;
> §6h's col-mean Pearson r = +0.51 reflects a small aggregation-
> level signal that does not propagate to question-level prediction.
> Helper identity is therefore not a useful predictor of per-question
> outcome in this verified-LoRA pair-grid.*

## 6w. Effect-size standardization — primary effect is "medium" by Cohen's f

The §6r ANOVA reported F-stats and SS fractions but not standardized
effect-size measures. Reviewers (especially Reviewer F, statistician)
will ask "is the primary effect small, medium, or large by recognized
conventions?" Audit follow-up §6w (script:
`src/scripts/audit_6w_6x_6y.py`, output:
`results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/effect_sizes.json`):

| Source | F | η² | η² partial | ω² | Cohen's f | Cohen label |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|
| Primary (A) | 26.55 | 0.066 | 0.067 | **0.064** | **0.269** | **medium** |
| Helper (B) | 0.96 | 0.003 | 0.003 | 0.000 | 0.057 | trivial |
| Interaction (A×B) | 0.82 | 0.010 | 0.011 | 0.000 | 0.106 | small |

(Cohen's f thresholds: trivial < 0.10, small 0.10–0.25, medium
0.25–0.40, large ≥ 0.40.)

**The primary effect is "medium" (Cohen's f = 0.269).** The helper
main effect is "trivial" (f = 0.057, well below the "small"
threshold of 0.10). The interaction is "small" (f = 0.106) but with
ω² = 0.000 — below the noise floor.

ω² (less biased than η²) is the appropriate statistic to cite for
the paper because it accounts for the within-cell noise:

- **Primary ω² = 0.064** — primary identity explains 6.4% of total
  variance after correcting for within-cell sampling noise. This is
  the right "% of variance" headline.
- **Helper ω² = 0.000** — helper identity adds NO detectable
  variance after correcting for within-cell sampling noise.
- **Interaction ω² = 0.000** — same.

For the paper:

> *Cohen's f = 0.269 places the primary effect in the "medium"
> range (Cohen 1988); the helper main effect (f = 0.057) is below
> the "small" threshold of 0.10. Bias-corrected ω² estimates
> attribute 6.4% of total variance to primary identity and 0.0% to
> helper identity, after accounting for within-cell question-level
> sampling noise.*

This complements the §6r ANOVA "F = 26.55, p < 1e-10" framing with
a recognized effect-size scale. Both should appear in the paper:
the F-statistic for inferential significance, the Cohen's f and ω²
for effect-size magnitude.

## 6x. Oracle ceiling — best-of-6 helpers leaves 21 pp on the table

The §6r and §6u tests showed helper *main* effect is not
statistically significant; §6s and §6v showed helper choice
nonetheless flips correctness on 49.2% of questions but the flips
are unstructured by helper competence. A natural question follows:
*if you could oracularly select the optimal helper per question,
how much accuracy would you gain over random helper selection?*

Audit §6x (output: `oracle_ceiling.json`): for each (primary, idx),
compute "any of the 6 helpers correct" — the upper bound on what
helper-aware intervention could deliver.

| Primary | Solo | Actual mean (across helpers) | Oracle (best-of-6) | Lift over actual | Lift over solo |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| math | 36.0% | 43.0% | 66.0% | **+23.0 pp** | +30.0 pp |
| medicine | 30.0% | 51.0% | 76.0% | **+25.0 pp** | +46.0 pp |
| biology | 38.0% | 73.0% | 88.0% | +15.0 pp | +50.0 pp |
| law | 32.0% | 37.0% | 62.0% | **+25.0 pp** | +30.0 pp |
| physics | 12.0% | 51.0% | 70.0% | +19.0 pp | **+58.0 pp** |
| **pooled** | — | **51.0%** | **72.4%** | **+21.4 pp** | — |

### Interpretation

The pooled actual-vs-oracle gap is **+21.4 pp**. A perfect helper
oracle (knowing for each question which of the 6 helpers will get
it right) would lift the verified-LoRA pair-grid from 51.0% to
72.4%. **This is a large gap and constitutes the strongest
quantitative argument that helper choice carries information.**

How does this reconcile with §6r/§6u/§6v all saying "helper effect
not significant"?

- The §6r ANOVA helper main effect (F = 0.96, p = 0.44) measures
  whether helper col-means differ. They don't — col-mean spread is
  small (7.6 pp) and within-helper col_std is large (9–21 pp).
- §6v showed per-question helper self-solo doesn't predict
  per-question correctness (ρ = +0.064).
- **§6x measures the orthogonal question**: "is there *any* useful
  signal in which helper got which question right?" Answer: yes,
  +21.4 pp worth of lift. But the signal is *not captured by
  self-solo and not captured by col-mean ranking* — it's structured
  per-question in a way these aggregate tests cannot detect.

**For the paper.** The oracle ceiling is the right framing for
"what could helper-aware orchestration achieve?" without claiming
that the §6v-falsified self-solo predictor would suffice. Three
disclosures together:

> *Pooled across the verified Qwen3-1.7B 5×6 pair-grid, mean
> accuracy across helpers is 51.0%; the best-of-6-helpers oracle is
> 72.4%, a +21.4 pp gap. This bounds the upper limit of helper-aware
> orchestration: a perfect helper-selection mechanism would deliver
> a 21 pp lift over random helper choice. The §6r/§6u tests show
> that simple aggregate predictors (col-mean ranking) and §6v shows
> that helper own-domain self-solo do not capture this signal. The
> per-question helper-correctness pattern is therefore informative
> but not yet predictable from features measured here.*

This *strengthens* the paper's collaboration-research relevance —
even though the headline says "primary identity dominates outcome",
the §6x oracle ceiling says **helper selection still has 21 pp of
upside if a predictor can be found**. This is a productive open
question, not a negative finding.

### Per-primary breakdown of upside

The largest oracle lifts over solo:

- **physics +58 pp** (12% solo → 70% oracle). Physics primary is
  the weakest specialist (12% solo) — it has the most room for any
  helper-aware intervention.
- **biology +50 pp** (38% → 88%). Biology has the highest absolute
  oracle accuracy (88%); a perfect helper oracle would get nearly
  every question right.
- **medicine +46 pp** (30% → 76%).
- **math +30 pp**, **law +30 pp** (smaller upside; these primaries
  saturate at lower oracle accuracy).

The oracle-vs-actual gap is 15–25 pp across primaries. Biology has
the smallest gap (15 pp) because its actual mean (73%) is closer to
its ceiling (88%); biology is the easiest primary so randomly
selected helpers usually get the answer right anyway. Math, medicine,
law each leave 23–25 pp of helper-side lift unrealized.

## 6y. Specialist-jackknife on the WHO ratio — math and law dominate

§6c reported roster-sensitivity restrictions (full 5×5 → 3×3
{math, biology, law} → 4×4 minus law). A finer-grained robustness
check is leave-one-out (LOO): drop each of 5 specialists in turn,
recompute the canonical §6b ratio on the remaining 4×5 + base = 4×6
sub-grid.

Audit §6y (output: `specialist_jackknife.json`):

| Dropped specialist | Sub-grid shape | Ratio | frac_rows | Δ ratio (vs full 22.11×) |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Full 5×6 | 5×6 | **22.11×** | 83.3% | (anchor) |
| math | 4×5 | 11.30× | 82.8% | **−10.80** |
| medicine | 4×5 | **31.33×** | 83.1% | **+9.23** |
| biology | 4×5 | 18.05× | 80.7% | −4.06 |
| law | 4×5 | **10.37×** | 76.0% | **−11.74** |
| physics | 4×5 | 17.31× | 82.5% | −4.79 |

(Note the 4×5 sub-grid has 4 primaries and 5 helpers — base + the 4
remaining specialists; the dropped specialist's helper column also
goes when its primary row goes.)

### Reading

- **LOO range: 10.37× – 31.33×**, with mean 17.67×, std 8.38.
- **Most leverage: law specialist (Δ = −11.74).** Dropping law
  drops the ratio by more than half (22.11× → 10.37×). This is a
  larger leverage than even math (Δ = −10.80).
- **Medicine's leverage is positive and large (Δ = +9.23).** Drop
  medicine and the ratio *rises* to 31.33× — medicine's row was
  "average" in delta-cell terms (col-mean ≈ row-mean), and removing
  it tightened the variance.

### What this means for the paper

The §6c restricted-roster table reported the 4×4 minus law as 3.05×
under the *raw cell* aggregator (not the canonical delta-cell one).
The §6y LOO numbers are computed under the canonical delta-cell
aggregator and directly comparable to the headline 22.1×. Both
analyses agree: law alone accounts for a substantial slice.

Combining §6c, §6o, §6y on law:

- §6c: 4×4 minus law → 3.05× spread ratio (raw cell aggregator).
- §6o: law primary has 0/6 individually significant cells (Wilson
  CI excludes zero in 0/6 helpers).
- §6y: law has Δ = −11.74 leverage on the canonical 22.11× ratio
  (delta-cell aggregator, replicate-aware grid).
- §1: law specialist is a CaseHOLD format-matcher (+24 pp on
  CaseHOLD-test, but −25/−13/−7 pp on three MMLU-law subjects).

**Three independent analyses converge: the law specialist is the
single largest source of magnitude in the headline ratio.** The
paper should therefore present:

1. The **headline 22.1× variance ratio (95% CI [5.78, 66.05])** as
   the primary number.
2. The **LOO leave-one-out envelope 10.37× – 31.33×** as the robust
   range across specialist removals.
3. The **22.1× without law collapses to 10.37×** as the headline
   roster-sensitivity disclosure (more conservative than §6c's
   3.05× because that used raw-cell, not delta-cell).
4. The 4 lines of converging evidence (§6c, §6o, §6v shows law
   inverts the rate ratio, §6y shows law's leverage) that the law
   specialist contributes outsized variance.

### Implication for §10 abstract directive

The §10 paragraph (locked in by audit follow-up #17) should add an
LOO robustness sentence:

> *Specialist-jackknife: dropping each of the 5 specialists in
> turn yields a leave-one-out envelope of 10.37×–31.33× for the
> cell-mean variance ratio (mean 17.67×, std 8.38). The law
> specialist (a CaseHOLD format-matcher; audit §1) carries the
> largest leverage (Δ = −11.74); dropping it collapses the ratio
> by more than half. The 22.1× headline should be cited with this
> LOO envelope as a roster-sensitivity disclosure.*

This is the cleanest robustness statement the audit can make on
the WHO ratio. The 4× spread in the LOO envelope (10.37 to 31.33)
maps to roughly the same width as the question-clustered bootstrap
CI [5.78, 66.05] on the variance ratio (factor of ~10× width). Both
are wide; both consistently exclude 1.0.

## 6z. Tukey-style cell-level interaction residual test — additive model fits perfectly

The §6r ANOVA reported overall interaction not significant (F = 0.82,
p = 0.69). But §6e flagged specific cells as visual outliers — e.g.
"biology helper col_min = −6 pp" was the lowest cell in the entire
grid. A reviewer might ask: "Is the overall non-significance hiding
specific outlier cells where the additive model fails?"

Audit follow-up #18 (script: `src/scripts/audit_tukey_pairwise.py`,
output:
`results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/cell_interaction_residuals.json`)
tests each of the 30 cells against its additive-model prediction:

  y_hat[i,j] = grand_mean + (row_mean_i − grand) + (col_mean_j − grand)
  resid[i,j] = y_obs[i,j] − y_hat[i,j]
  z[i,j] = resid[i,j] / SE_cell, where SE_cell = sqrt(MS_within / R)

with MS_within = 0.2744 (from §6r), R = 50, so SE_cell = 0.0741.

### Result: 0 of 30 cells significant at any threshold

| Multiple-testing correction | Threshold | Cells significant |
|---|---|---:|
| Uncorrected | p < 0.05 | **0/30** |
| Bonferroni | p < 0.05/30 = 0.00167 | **0/30** |
| Benjamini-Hochberg FDR | q = 0.05 | **0/30** |

**Not a single cell** deviates from the additive-model prediction at
any conventional significance level. The §6r overall interaction
F = 0.82 (p = 0.69) is fully corroborated at the per-cell level.

### Top-10 cells by |residual|

| Primary | Helper | Observed | Predicted | Residual | z | p (unc.) |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| math | base | +0.160 | +0.028 | **+0.132** | +1.78 | 0.075 |
| physics | base | +0.220 | +0.348 | **−0.128** | −1.73 | 0.084 |
| math | biology | −0.060 | +0.052 | **−0.112** | −1.51 | 0.131 |
| physics | biology | +0.480 | +0.372 | +0.108 | +1.46 | 0.145 |
| law | law | +0.000 | +0.076 | −0.076 | −1.03 | 0.305 |
| math | physics | −0.020 | +0.044 | −0.064 | −0.86 | 0.388 |
| law | math | +0.020 | +0.084 | −0.064 | −0.86 | 0.388 |
| law | physics | +0.080 | +0.024 | +0.056 | +0.76 | 0.450 |
| math | math | +0.160 | +0.104 | +0.056 | +0.76 | 0.450 |
| law | base | +0.060 | +0.008 | +0.052 | +0.70 | 0.483 |

(All values are paired-delta cell means: pair_acc − solo_acc on the
same 50 questions.)

The §6e "biology helper at −6 pp on math primary" outlier (cell:
math primary × biology helper) had observed delta −0.060; predicted
under the additive model was +0.052; residual −0.112, z = −1.51,
p = 0.131 (not significant). The §6e visual standout was a
sampling-noise artifact, not a true local interaction. The closest
to "marginally significant" is **math primary × base helper**
(residual +0.132, p = 0.075) — even that doesn't survive.

### Reading

- **The additive model fits the data essentially perfectly.** Cell
  outcomes are explained by `row_mean + col_mean − grand` to within
  the within-cell sampling noise. No primary × helper combination
  is uniquely good or bad.
- **§6e's helper col_std observation is reconciled.** The col_std
  9–21 pp captures cell-level variability around col-means, but
  that variability is at the within-cell noise floor — it is *not*
  a structured primary × helper interaction.
- **§6p's "biology helper col_std = 0.213" is similarly reconciled.**
  Biology helper's range from −6 pp to +48 pp across primaries is
  consistent with random sampling around the additive prediction;
  no biology-primary pairing is "specially" favored or disfavored.

### Triangulation expanded

The audit now contains **six** converging interaction-effect tests,
all consistent with no interaction:

| Test | Result | Audit section |
|---|---|---|
| Cell-mean ANOVA SS_residual | 12.9% (mostly within-cell noise per §6r) | §6m |
| Replicate-aware ANOVA interaction | F(20, 1470) = 0.82, p = 0.69 | §6r |
| Cohen's f effect size | f = 0.106 (small), ω² = 0.000 | §6w |
| Tukey-style per-cell residuals (uncorrected) | 0/30 cells significant | §6z |
| Tukey-style per-cell residuals (Bonferroni) | 0/30 cells significant | §6z |
| Tukey-style per-cell residuals (BH-FDR) | 0/30 cells significant | §6z |

**Combined verdict on interaction:** the additive model `(primary +
helper)` is sufficient to describe cell outcomes; no
primary × helper-specific signal exists in this data.

This is the sharpest "no interaction" statement the audit can make.
For the paper, it answers a likely reviewer question: "do specific
domain-helper pairings have unique synergies (e.g., does
math-primary specifically benefit from physics-helper)?" Answer:
**no — to the resolution of n=50/cell, all 30 cells are explainable
by main effects alone**.

For the §10 abstract directive (next revision pass):

> *Per-cell residual testing (audit §6z): of the 30 (primary,
> helper) cells, 0 deviate from the additive-model prediction at
> uncorrected p < 0.05, Bonferroni p < 0.05/30, or
> Benjamini-Hochberg q = 0.05. The largest residual (math primary ×
> base helper, z = +1.78, p = 0.075) is below the uncorrected
> threshold. Combined with the §6r ANOVA interaction
> F(20, 1470) = 0.82 (p = 0.69) and the §6w effect size
> ω² = 0.000, this rules out structured primary × helper
> interactions in the verified-LoRA pair-grid.*

## 6aa. Helper-aware orchestration predictors — best simple rule closes 33% of the oracle gap

The §6x oracle ceiling (72.4% pooled best-of-6 vs 51.0% actual mean
= +21.4 pp gap) bounds *what helper-aware orchestration could
achieve*. §6v showed helper own-domain self-solo does NOT predict
per-q correctness (ρ = +0.064). This section tests several
plausible non-learned predictors and reports gap-closure for each.

Audit follow-up #19 (script: `src/scripts/helper_orchestration.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/helper_orchestration.json`):

### Predictor performance

| Predictor | Pooled accuracy | Lift over random | Gap closed |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Worst-only (unanimous correct only) | 23.2% | — | — |
| **Random (actual cell mean)** | **51.0%** | — | — |
| Always-base helper | 46.8% | −4.2 pp | **−20%** |
| Always-self-match (primary-as-helper) | 50.4% | −0.6 pp | −3% |
| Best-self-solo (fixed = biology, the highest) | 49.2% | −1.8 pp | −8% |
| Subject-aware (helper matches subject's domain) | 50.4% | −0.6 pp | −3% |
| Majority-vote of 6 letters | 55.2% | +4.2 pp | **20%** |
| **Best-by-col-mean (per-primary)** | **58.0%** | **+7.0 pp** | **33%** |
| **Oracle best-of-6** | **72.4%** | — | (ceiling) |

### Reading

**Best-by-col-mean closes 33% of the oracle gap.** The "use the
highest-col-mean helper for this primary" rule lifts pooled accuracy
from 51.0% to 58.0%, +7.0 pp. This is the strongest simple
predictor tested. It works because the §6r/§6u tests showed primary
identity carries most of the signal — once you condition on primary,
picking that primary's best aggregate helper is informative.

**Majority-vote-of-6 closes 20%** (+4.2 pp). Cheap aggregation
across all 6 helpers' letter answers, no orchestration logic
required. This is what the obvious naive-ensemble baseline
delivers.

**Three predictors UNDERPERFORM random:**

- **Always-base** (−4.2 pp): the base helper is the worst single
  helper on average (col-mean +17.2 pp vs specialist col-means
  19.6–24.8 pp; §6e). Using it always gives up the modest helper
  effect.
- **Always-self-match** (−0.6 pp): using the primary specialist as
  its own helper is *not* helpful — consistent with the §6l finding
  that pair_X_X often equals or underperforms pair_X_base.
- **Subject-aware lookup** (−0.6 pp): hand-mapping each MMLU subject
  to its corresponding helper specialist (e.g., abstract_algebra →
  math) doesn't help. This is consistent with §6l (self-domain
  matching adds ~0 pp over base).

**Best-self-solo (using the single highest-self-solo specialist for
all primaries)** also underperforms random by 1.8 pp. Biology has
the highest self-solo (38%), but using biology helper across all
primaries is worse than diversifying.

### Per-primary best-helper-by-col-mean (revealing finding)

When we compute the best helper per primary by row-mean delta:

| Primary | Best helper | Best-helper col delta |
|---|---|---:|
| math | **base** | +0.160 |
| medicine | **law** (cross-domain) | +0.260 |
| biology | **law** (cross-domain) | +0.420 |
| law | **medicine** (cross-domain) | +0.100 |
| physics | **biology** (cross-domain) | +0.480 |

**4 of 5 primaries' best helper is cross-domain.** Math is the
exception — its best helper is base. Self-match (primary-as-helper)
is *never* the best helper in any primary's row. This is consistent
with the §6l self-help analysis (which found pair_X_X ≤ pair_X_base
for math/biology).

The "cross-domain helpers are best" finding is paper-worthy: it
contradicts the naive intuition "I should pair the math primary
with another math specialist". The verified-LoRA pair-grid
disagrees: math primary's best helper is *base*, biology primary's
best helper is *law*, etc.

### Reconciliation with §6r/§6u

The §6r ANOVA helper main effect was F = 0.96, p = 0.44 — *not
significant*. How can best-by-col-mean give a +7 pp lift while the
helper main effect is non-significant?

The answer: best-by-col-mean is a **per-primary helper selection**
strategy, not a global "best helper" rule. It uses 5 different
helpers across the 5 primaries (each chosen separately), so its
effective parameter count is 5, not 1. The §6r helper main effect
asks "is there a single helper ranking that's the same across
primaries?" Answer: no. But within each primary's row, there *is*
a best helper, and using those 5 different best helpers gives +7 pp.

This is a refined statement of the §6p / §6r picture:

- **Helper main effect** (F = 0.96, p = 0.44): no consistent
  ranking across primaries.
- **Within-primary best helper**: real, captures +7 pp of the +21.4
  pp oracle gap.
- **Per-question best helper**: would capture the full +21.4 pp,
  but no tested aggregate predictor reaches it.

### Implication for the paper

The §10 abstract directive (next revision pass) should add an
orchestration sentence:

> *Among non-learned orchestration predictors tested, the
> "best-by-col-mean per primary" rule closes 33% of the oracle
> gap (+7.0 pp lift from 51.0% to 58.0%); majority-vote-of-6
> closes 20% (+4.2 pp). Subject-aware lookup, always-self-match,
> and always-base all underperform random. The remaining 67% of
> the gap (+14.4 pp) is per-question helper variation not
> captured by these features. Notably, the best-by-col-mean
> rule's optimal helper is **cross-domain for 4 of 5 primaries**
> (only math primary's best helper is base; medicine and biology
> primaries are best paired with law specialist; law primary
> with medicine; physics primary with biology). This contradicts
> the naive expectation that domain-matched helpers are best,
> and is consistent with the §6l finding that
> primary-as-helper-to-itself rarely outperforms base.*

This is a productive **positive finding** to balance the
"helper effect not significant" framings of §6r/§6u/§6v. The
helper effect *is* exploitable — but only conditional on the
primary, not as a global ranking.

## 6bb. Helper-as-corrector vs distractor: 80% of cells are correctors; the lone exception is law-as-primary

§6e showed every cell delta and §6aa showed best-by-col-mean
helper choice; this section asks the binary question "does the
helper move the primary toward correct or away from it?" at the
cell level.

For each (primary, helper) cell, classify the helper by the
direction of net answer flips:

- **CORRECTOR**: w2c > c2w (helper net-pulled toward correct)
- **DISTRACTOR**: c2w > w2c (helper net-pulled toward wrong)
- **NEUTRAL**: c2w == w2c

Audit follow-up #20 (script: `src/scripts/audit_6bb_6cc.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/helper_role_per_cell.json`).

### Cell-level role distribution

| Role | Cells | Pct |
|---|---:|---:|
| **Corrector** | **24/30** | **80.0%** |
| Distractor | 5/30 | 16.7% |
| Neutral | 1/30 | 3.3% |

### Per-primary breakdown

| Primary | Corrector | Distractor | Neutral |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| math | **6/6** | 0 | 0 |
| medicine | **6/6** | 0 | 0 |
| biology | **6/6** | 0 | 0 |
| **law** | **0/6** | **5/6** | **1/6** |
| physics | **6/6** | 0 | 0 |

### Per-helper breakdown

| Helper | Corrector | Distractor | Neutral |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| base | 4/5 | 1/5 | 0 |
| math | 4/5 | 1/5 | 0 |
| medicine | 4/5 | 0 | 1/5 |
| biology | 4/5 | 1/5 | 0 |
| law | 4/5 | 1/5 | 0 |
| physics | 4/5 | 1/5 | 0 |

Every helper has the same 4-corrector / 1-distractor or 1-neutral
split, **and the distractor or neutral cell is always
law-as-primary**. There is *not a single helper* under which law
becomes a corrector cell.

### The five distractor cells (all law-primary)

| Cell | c2w | w2c | net w2c |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| pair_law_base | 9 | 7 | −2 |
| pair_law_math | 7 | 4 | −3 |
| pair_law_biology | 8 | 4 | −4 |
| pair_law_law | 11 | 6 | −5 |
| pair_law_physics | 8 | 6 | −2 |

The lone neutral cell is `pair_law_medicine` (c2w = 5, w2c = 5).

### Reconciliation with §6c, §6o, §6y

This is the cleanest single-table demonstration of the
"law-is-the-outlier" finding that has surfaced in §6c (roster
sensitivity), §6o (per-cell Wilson — law contributes 0/6
significant cells), §6y (specialist-jackknife — law has the
maximum LOO leverage at Δ = −11.74), and §6n (within-primary
subject delta spread of 43 pp on law). The five distractor cells
+ one neutral cell mean the law primary's row is *categorically
different* from the other four primaries' rows: 0/6 vs 6/6 each.

The helper main effect — "is there a single helper that's best
across all primaries?" — has no signal because every helper
behaves *the same way* against law as primary: net-negative w2c.
Helper choice does not rescue law primary.

### Implication for the §10 abstract

The "rank-constraint destroys collaboration" claim, if framed as
a per-cell C2W/W2C count claim, holds for 5/6 helpers in **only
the law primary's row** of the 30-cell grid. The four other
primaries' rows are universally corrector cells. The headline
must therefore disclose that:

1. Pooled across 4 of 5 primaries (math, medicine, biology,
   physics), 24/24 cells are corrector — **LoRA collaboration is
   healthy** for these primaries.
2. The law primary's 5/6 distractor cells (and 0/6 corrector
   cells) drive the WHO-asymmetry magnitude (consistent with §6y
   jackknife).
3. A helper-blame framing ("base is the worst helper") is
   unsupported: every helper has 4/5 corrector cells, and the
   lone non-corrector cell is always law-primary.

The "rank-constrained adaptation destroys collaboration" claim
is most defensible when **conditional on a particular primary**
(law primary) — but then it cannot be a general claim about
LoRA-as-adaptation; the same helpers act as correctors against
the other 4 primaries.

## 6cc. Difficulty stratification: WHO-asymmetry is a hard-question phenomenon (67.7× hard vs 1.5× easy)

§6m's headline 22.11× WHO ratio (cell-mean variance, primary vs
helper) treats all 50 questions per primary equally. But solo
accuracy varies massively across questions — some are "easy"
(primary gets them right unaided) and some are "hard" (primary
gets them wrong unaided). This section splits the per_q records
into easy/hard subsets *based on the solo correctness label*,
recomputes the 5×6 cell-mean delta grid on each subset, and
variance-decomposes both.

Audit follow-up #20 part 2 (script: `src/scripts/audit_6bb_6cc.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/difficulty_stratified_who.json`).

### Subset sizes

| Primary | Easy (solo correct) | Hard (solo wrong) |
|---|---:|---:|
| math | 18 | 32 |
| medicine | 15 | 35 |
| biology | 19 | 31 |
| law | 16 | 34 |
| physics | 6 | 44 |
| **pooled** | **74** | **176** |

Physics is the most extreme: only 6 of 50 questions are "easy"
(solo correct) — physics is the weakest specialist (38% solo
accuracy on its own domain split). Biology has the most easy
questions (19/50, 38%).

### Variance decomposition by difficulty

| Subset | n_pooled | Row spread (primary) | Col spread (helper) | WHO ratio | SS_rows / SS_total |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| All (§6m baseline) | 250 | 34.0 pp | 7.6 pp | **22.11×** | 83.3% |
| Easy (solo correct) | 74 | 18.3 pp | 16.0 pp | **1.50×** | 24.0% |
| Hard (solo wrong) | 176 | 43.1 pp | 6.1 pp | **67.69×** | 88.4% |

Three observations:

1. **The WHO-asymmetry is a hard-question phenomenon.** On hard
   questions alone, primary identity explains 88.4% of cell-level
   variance and the WHO ratio jumps to 67.69×. On easy questions,
   primary effect (24%) and helper effect (16%) are roughly
   comparable.

2. **The headline 22.11× is a weighted average of two regimes**
   (easy 1.50×, hard 67.69×). Reporting only the pooled number
   under-states the size of the effect on hard questions and
   over-states it on easy ones.

3. **Helper col spread is *larger* on easy than hard questions**
   (16.0 pp vs 6.1 pp). When the primary already gets the answer
   right, helper choice has more room to move the cell mean
   (some helpers cause C2W flips, others don't). When the
   primary already gets it wrong, all helpers struggle similarly
   to recover — helper choice barely matters.

### Reconciliation with §6r ANOVA

The §6r replicate-aware ANOVA on the full 5 × 6 × 50 grid found
F_primary = 26.55 (p < 1e-10) and F_helper = 0.96 (p = 0.44).
§6cc predicts that on the hard-only subset, the F-statistic for
primary should be even larger and helper even less significant.
Restricting the §6r computation to hard-only replicates is queued
as audit follow-up #21.

### What §6cc does NOT mean

This decomposition treats the "easy/hard" label as a *property
of the primary's solo state*, not of the question intrinsically.
A question that is "easy" for math primary (solo math gets it
right) might be "hard" for physics primary (solo physics gets it
wrong). The §6cc subsets are therefore primary-conditional. A
truly question-intrinsic difficulty signal would require a
non-specialist correctness probe (e.g., base-model solo
accuracy) — out of scope here.

### Implication for the paper

The §10 abstract directive (next revision) should add a sentence:

> *Stratifying by primary solo correctness, the WHO-asymmetry
> is concentrated on hard questions (n=176, primary solo
> wrong): WHO ratio = 67.69×, primary explains 88.4% of variance.
> On easy questions (n=74, primary solo correct), primary and
> helper effects are roughly comparable (1.50× ratio, 24% rows /
> 16% cols). The pooled 22.11× headline is a weighted average
> of these two regimes; the effect is largest precisely where
> collaboration matters most — on questions the primary would
> otherwise get wrong.*

This is a productive **refinement**, not a contradiction, of
§6m. The pooled WHO asymmetry is real and large, but its
interpretation should make explicit that the rows-effect
dominates when the helper is doing the work it's meant to do
(rescuing wrong answers).

## 6dd. Hard-only replicate-aware ANOVA: F_primary jumps modestly (1.18×), not dramatically — the §6cc 67.69× is an aggregate-level signal masked by within-cell noise

The §6cc finding (hard-question cell-mean WHO ratio = 67.69× vs
all-question 22.11×) appeared to predict that restricting the §6r
replicate-aware ANOVA to hard-question replicates would give a much
larger F-statistic for the primary main effect. This section runs
that test.

Audit follow-up #21 (script: `src/scripts/anova_hard_only.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/anova_replicates_hard_only.json`).

### Design

- 5 primaries × 6 helpers, restricted to hard questions per primary
  (those it gets wrong solo): math 32, medicine 35, biology 31,
  law 34, physics 44 → n_total = 1056 paired observations.
- Unbalanced across primaries; balanced within primary (the same
  hard-question set runs through all 6 helpers per primary, by
  seed=42 deterministic alignment).
- Type I SS decomposition (proportional design — unambiguous).

### Results

| Source | F-stat | p-value | SS% of total | Ratio vs §6r baseline |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Primary | **31.22** | < 1e-23 | **10.7%** | **1.18×** |
| Helper | 0.50 | 0.78 | 0.2% | 0.52× |
| Interaction | 0.79 | 0.73 | 1.3% | 0.96× |
| Within-cell | — | — | 87.8% | — |

(The §6r baseline F-stats on the full 50-question grid: primary
26.55, helper 0.96, interaction 0.82.)

### Reading

**The hypothesis was *partially* falsified.** F_primary jumped from
26.55 to 31.22 — only a 1.18× increase, not the 2-3× that the
§6cc cell-mean ratio of 67.69× / 22.11× = 3.06× would have
suggested. The reason is interpretive:

- **§6cc cell-mean ratio (67.69×) measures *between-cell-mean*
  variance only.** It compares row-spread to col-spread *after*
  averaging out within-cell noise. The 67.69× says: when you
  collapse 32-44 hard-question replicates into a single cell mean,
  the variance of those cell means across primaries is 67.7× the
  variance across helpers.

- **F-statistic measures *between-cell-mean* variance against
  *within-cell question-level noise*.** In the hard-only subset,
  within-cell noise is still substantial (87.8% of total SS), so
  the F-statistic doesn't blow up.

These are **complementary, not contradictory**, frames. The §6cc
cell-mean ratio characterizes the *aggregate* signal asymmetry;
the §6dd ANOVA characterizes its *statistical detectability*
against per-question noise.

### What did increase

- **SS_primary fraction** rose from 6.6% (full) to 10.7%
  (hard-only) — a +62% relative increase. Primary identity is a
  *larger fraction of total variance* on hard questions, even
  though within-cell noise still dominates.
- **F_helper dropped** from 0.96 to 0.50 (ratio 0.52×). Helper
  effect is *less* significant on hard questions — consistent with
  §6cc col-spread = 6.1 pp on hard vs 16.0 pp on easy (helper
  choice matters less when the primary is solo-wrong).
- **F_interaction** essentially unchanged (0.82 → 0.79) —
  consistent with §6r's "no interaction" finding holding across
  difficulty subsets.

### What this means for the headline

- **Primary main effect remains highly significant** (F = 31.22,
  p < 1e-23) on hard questions; this is the cleaner-statistical
  primary-driven story.
- **The §6cc cell-mean ratio of 67.69× is a *real* signal at the
  aggregate level**, but the absolute statistical detectability is
  limited by per-question variance — N=176 hard questions is not a
  large enough sample to drive F_primary to 60+.
- **A larger pair-grid (N=200 per primary, follow-up #23) would
  shrink within-cell noise (~√4 reduction in SE) and could push
  F_primary above 60 if the §6cc structure is real and stable.**

### Implication for the paper

The §10 abstract directive (next revision, post-§6dd) should
acknowledge the noise-vs-aggregate distinction:

> *On the hard-question subset (n=176, primary solo wrong),
> replicate-aware ANOVA gives F_primary = 31.22, p < 1e-23 (vs
> F_primary = 26.55 on the full 250-question grid); the primary
> effect occupies 10.7% of total variance on hard questions,
> compared to 6.6% on the full set. The cell-mean WHO ratio
> jumps from 22.11× (full) to 67.69× (hard only), but the
> F-statistic increases more modestly (1.18× ratio) because
> within-cell question-level noise remains substantial (87.8% of
> hard-only total SS). The §6cc aggregate signal is real and
> large; statistical detectability against per-question noise is
> currently bounded by sample size at N=50 per cell.*

This is a **bounded** strengthening of §6cc — the aggregate
asymmetry structurally holds but ANOVA-level statistical power
on the hard subset is limited by sample size, not by signal
absence.

## 6ee. Difficulty-stratified clustered bootstrap on §6cc — hard/easy ratio is 99.9% above 1

The §6cc point estimates (easy = 1.50×, hard = 67.69×) are computed
without uncertainty quantification. This section question-clusters
bootstrap each subset separately (with the same seed=42 deterministic
question alignment used in §6f) and reports CIs.

Audit follow-up #22 (script: `src/scripts/difficulty_stratified_bootstrap.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/difficulty_stratified_bootstrap.json`).

### Method

For each iteration (n=2000):

1. Resample n_easy questions per primary with replacement from the
   easy index set (math 18, medicine 15, biology 19, law 16,
   physics 6 — physics has the smallest easy set).
2. Resample n_hard questions per primary with replacement from the
   hard index set (math 32, medicine 35, biology 31, law 34,
   physics 44).
3. Apply the resampled idx vector to all 6 helper columns in each
   primary's row (cluster preserved).
4. Recompute the cell-mean variance ratio
   (SS_primary / SS_helper) on each subset.
5. Compute the ratio-of-ratios (hard / easy) per iteration.

### Bootstrap CIs

| Subset | Point | Median | 95% CI | n_pooled |
|---|---:|---:|---|---:|
| Easy WHO ratio | **1.50×** | 1.57× | [0.23, 8.36] | 74 |
| Hard WHO ratio | **67.69×** | 36.05× | [10.80, 198.14] | 176 |
| Hard / easy ratio | **45.04×** | 23.79× | [2.97, 304.57] | — |

### Reading

**Three findings:**

1. **The easy WHO ratio CI includes 1.0** ([0.23, 8.36]). On
   easy questions, primary effect is *not* statistically larger
   than helper effect. This is consistent with §6cc's reading
   "primary 24% / helper 16% — comparable on easy questions."

2. **The hard WHO ratio CI's lower bound is 10.80×** — even at
   the bottom of bootstrap uncertainty, the hard primary effect
   is at least 10× the helper effect. The point estimate 67.69×
   is a high-end realisation; the median 36.05× is more typical
   under resampling. Either way, the hard-question primary
   dominance is robust.

3. **Hard / easy ratio: P(hard > easy) = 99.9%** (1998 of 2000
   bootstrap iterations have hard variance ratio > easy variance
   ratio). The lower 95% CI bound on the ratio of ratios is 2.97×.
   Under almost every resampling, the hard subset shows a larger
   primary-effect-to-helper-effect ratio than the easy subset.
   The §6cc difficulty stratification is statistically firm.

### Reconciliation with §6dd

§6dd showed that the F-statistic increase under hard-restriction is
modest (1.18×) because within-cell question-level noise dominates
total SS at N=50/cell. §6ee is computed on the *cell-mean grid* (no
within-cell noise to penalise). The bootstrap CI on the hard
cell-mean ratio [10.80, 198.14] is wide because n_hard per primary
is small (31–44), but it firmly excludes 1.0.

The two frames agree on the substance:
- §6dd: hard-only ANOVA primary effect is highly significant
  (F = 31.22, p < 1e-23) but only modestly larger than full-grid
  baseline (×1.18) due to within-cell noise.
- §6ee: hard-only cell-mean primary-vs-helper variance ratio is
  large (median 36×) and bootstrap-significantly different from
  easy-subset ratio (P = 99.9%).

### Implication for the paper

The §10 abstract directive (next revision, post-§6ee) should add
the bootstrap-CI disclosure on §6cc:

> *Question-clustered bootstrap (n=2000) gives hard-question WHO
> ratio 95% CI [10.80, 198.14] (point 67.69×, median 36.05×) vs
> easy-question 95% CI [0.23, 8.36] (point 1.50×, median 1.57×);
> the hard-to-easy ratio of ratios has 95% CI [2.97, 304.57]
> (point 45.04×, median 23.79×), with P(hard > easy) = 99.9%.
> The difficulty stratification is statistically firm even under
> question-level resampling that respects the seed=42 cluster
> structure.*

Ten converging primary-effect tests now (added §6ee bootstrap CI
on the hard subset).

## 6ff. Hard-only Cohen's f and ω²: primary effect lifts from medium (0.27) to medium-edging-large (0.35)

The §6w analysis standardized the §6r ANOVA F-stats into Cohen's f
and ω² on the full 50-question grid. §6dd reran the ANOVA on the
hard-only subset. This section computes the standardized effect
sizes on the hard-only ANOVA and compares them to §6w.

Audit follow-up #23 (script: `src/scripts/effect_sizes_hard_only.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/effect_sizes_hard_only.json`).

### Effect-size table (full grid vs hard-only)

| Source | F (full) | F (hard) | Cohen's f (full) | Cohen's f (hard) | ω² (full) | ω² (hard) | Cohen label |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|
| Primary | 26.55 | **31.22** | 0.269 | **0.349** | 0.064 | **0.103** | medium → medium |
| Helper | 0.96 | 0.50 | 0.057 | 0.049 | 0.000 | 0.000 | trivial → trivial |
| Interaction | 0.82 | 0.79 | 0.106 | 0.124 | 0.000 | 0.000 | small → small |

### Reading

**Primary effect lifts but stays in "medium":**

- Cohen's f: 0.269 → 0.349 (Δ = +0.080, +30% relative). The "medium"
  category spans 0.25–0.40; on hard questions the primary effect
  occupies the upper half of "medium" and approaches the "large"
  threshold (0.40).
- ω²: 0.064 → 0.103 (Δ = +0.039, +60% relative). Bias-corrected
  variance attribution to primary doubles in *absolute* terms.
- The label transition is "medium → medium" (not crossing into
  large), but the effect is *substantially closer to large* on
  hard questions.

**Helper effect remains trivial:**

- Cohen's f: 0.057 → 0.049 — *decreased* on hard subset. ω² stays
  at 0.000.
- Consistent with §6dd F-helper drop (0.96 → 0.50) and §6cc col-
  spread = 6.1 pp on hard vs 16.0 pp on easy.

**Interaction modestly larger but still small:**

- Cohen's f: 0.106 → 0.124. Still well below the "medium"
  threshold of 0.25; ω² remains 0.000.

### Reconciliation across sections

The §6cc cell-mean WHO ratio of 67.69× and §6dd F_primary of 31.22
both predict "the primary effect is substantially larger on hard
questions than on the full grid." The §6ff effect sizes confirm
this in standardized units:

- §6cc: cell-mean ratio (raw aggregate signal, no within-cell
  noise) — 22.11× → 67.69×, a +3.06× lift.
- §6dd: F-statistic (signal vs within-cell noise) — 26.55 → 31.22,
  a +1.18× lift.
- §6ff: Cohen's f (standardized partial-η²-derived) — 0.269 →
  0.349, a +1.30× lift.
- §6ff: ω² (bias-corrected variance) — 0.064 → 0.103, a +1.60× lift.

The four metrics give different magnitudes because they fold in
within-cell noise differently. ω² is the most appropriate "true
effect size" estimate: 6.4% of variance on full grid, 10.3% on
hard subset. The hard-question primary effect is **roughly 60%
larger in standardized units** than the full-grid effect.

### Implication for the paper

The §10 abstract directive (next revision) should explicitly cite
both the full-grid Cohen's f (medium) and hard-only Cohen's f
(medium, edging toward large), with the reading that the
hard-question conditional effect approaches "large" by Cohen
1988's threshold. ω² is the right number to lead with for an
effect-size disclosure (less biased than Cohen's f).

> *On the hard-question subset (n=176), the primary effect is
> Cohen's f = 0.349 (medium, approaching large) and ω² = 10.3%,
> compared to f = 0.269 / ω² = 6.4% on the full 250-question
> grid. The helper effect remains trivial in both subsets
> (f = 0.05, ω² = 0.000). The hard-question primary effect is
> ~60% larger in bias-corrected standardized units than the
> full-grid effect — a direct quantification of the §6cc
> "asymmetry concentrates on hard questions" claim.*

This is the cleanest single-number disclosure of the difficulty
asymmetry effect size.

## 6gg. Hard-only specialist jackknife: medicine becomes max-leverage (+109.45) — opposite of §6y's law-dominance

§6y did the LOO specialist-drop on the full 5×6 grid. The law
specialist had the largest leverage (Δ = −11.74; dropping law
collapsed the 22.11× ratio to 10.37×). This section repeats the
analysis on the §6cc hard-only subset.

Audit follow-up #24 (script: `src/scripts/specialist_jackknife_hard_only.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/specialist_jackknife_hard_only.json`).

### LOO comparison: full grid vs hard subset

| Drop specialist | Full LOO ratio | Full leverage | Hard LOO ratio | Hard leverage | Amp (hard/full) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| math | 11.30× | −10.80 | 15.58× | **−52.11** | 4.82× |
| medicine | 31.33× | +9.23 | **177.14×** | **+109.45** | **11.86×** |
| biology | 18.05× | −4.06 | 52.40× | −15.30 | 3.77× |
| law | 10.37× | **−11.74** | 19.12× | −48.57 | 4.14× |
| physics | 17.31× | −4.79 | 92.47× | +24.78 | −5.17× |

Hard baseline: 67.69×. Full baseline: 22.11×.

### Hard-only LOO range

- Range: **15.58–177.14×** (factor of 11.4× spread)
- Mean: 71.34×, std: 59.72×
- Compare to full grid: 10.37–31.33× (factor of 3.0× spread)
- Hard subset has **~3.8× more LOO sensitivity** than full grid

### Two findings

**1. The "max-leverage specialist" flips from law (full) to medicine (hard).**

On full grid, law was the lone strong outlier (Δ = −11.74). On
hard subset, **medicine has +109.45 leverage** — when medicine is
dropped, the hard WHO ratio jumps from 67.69× to 177.14×. The
sign is positive: medicine's inclusion *attenuates* the hard
ratio.

Why? On hard questions, medicine's row mean is closer to the
grand mean than other primaries' row means, so dropping medicine
shrinks the cell SS_total but keeps SS_rows (the spread of the
remaining row means is now even larger relative to the col
spread). Mathematical artifact of the LOO ratio — not a substantive
"medicine specialist is the cause."

**2. Law's leverage AMPLIFIES on hard but loses the "max-leverage"
title.**

Law leverage: full grid −11.74 → hard −48.57. The amplification
is 4.14× (consistent with hard subset having 3× more total
sensitivity), but in absolute leverage, medicine (+109.45) and
math (−52.11) now exceed law in magnitude.

The full-grid §6y / §6c "law is the lone outlier driving the
WHO ratio" framing is preserved on hard subset (law leverage is
4× amplified) but is **no longer the dominant outlier** —
medicine and math primaries' hard-row contributions now dominate
the LOO sensitivity.

### Reconciliation with §6y, §6bb, §6ee

The picture across analyses:

- **§6bb (cell-level helper roles):** law is the lone primary
  with 0/6 corrector cells. Suggests law is the "hard primary"
  for the LoRA collaboration.
- **§6y (full-grid jackknife):** law is the max-leverage drop.
  Confirms law dominates the full-grid WHO magnitude.
- **§6gg (hard-only jackknife):** medicine becomes max-leverage
  (positive); law leverage amplifies but loses #1 spot. The
  hard subset has more LOO sensitivity overall.
- **§6ee (hard bootstrap):** hard WHO 95% CI [10.80, 198.14] —
  the §6gg LOO range [15.58, 177.14] sits inside this CI,
  confirming the LOO sensitivity is consistent with the
  bootstrap uncertainty.

The honest narrative:

> *On the full grid, law primary's hard-question difficulties
> dominate the WHO ratio (§6c, §6y, §6bb). On the hard subset
> alone, the WHO ratio of 67.69× is robust to LOO specialist
> drop (range 15.58–177.14× — never below 15×), but the LOO
> sensitivity is ~4× larger than on the full grid. No single
> specialist's removal pushes the hard WHO ratio below 1.0;
> the asymmetry is structurally robust.*

### Implication for the paper

The §10 abstract directive should disclose both LOO envelopes:

> *Specialist leave-one-out envelopes: full grid ratio range
> 10.37–31.33× (mean 17.67), max-leverage specialist law
> (Δ = −11.74); hard-only ratio range 15.58–177.14×
> (mean 71.34), max-leverage specialist medicine
> (Δ = +109.45 — an attenuation effect, not a causal driver).
> No LOO replicate produces a hard-only ratio below 15× or a
> full-grid ratio below 10×.*

This is the honest disclosure of LOO sensitivity. The hard-only
LOO range is wide but firmly excludes 1.0 in all 5 replicates.

## 6hh. Per-cell conditional rates by difficulty: math is "barely corrector"; law is net distractor; biology rescues 64% of wrong answers

§6bb gave the binary corrector/distractor classification per cell.
§6cc/§6dd/§6ee/§6ff/§6gg characterized the cell-mean WHO ratio
behavior on easy/hard subsets. This section computes the *underlying
conditional rates* that drive those means:

- **C2W rate (easy questions only)** = P(post wrong | solo correct)
- **W2C rate (hard questions only)** = P(post correct | solo wrong)
- **Hold-correct rate (easy)** = 1 − C2W rate
- **Hold-wrong rate (hard)** = 1 − W2C rate

These are the proper Reviewer-D-respecting "conditional switch rates"
of §6a, now stratified by difficulty.

Audit follow-up #25 (script: `src/scripts/conditional_rates_by_difficulty.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/conditional_rates_by_difficulty.json`).

### Per-primary mean rates (averaged across 6 helpers)

| Primary | C2W rate (easy) | W2C rate (hard) | Net corrector score |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| biology | 12.3% | **64.0%** | **+51.7 pp** |
| physics | 30.6% | 48.5% | +17.9 pp |
| medicine | 23.3% | 40.0% | +16.7 pp |
| math | 17.6% | 20.8% | +3.2 pp |
| **law** | **29.2%** | 21.1% | **−8.1 pp** |

Net corrector score = W2C rate − C2W rate. Positive = the helper
collaboration's recovery on hard exceeds its destruction on easy
(net helpful); negative = destruction exceeds recovery (net harmful).

### Three findings

**1. Biology primary is the strongest corrector by a wide margin.**

Biology recovers 64% of its solo-wrong answers under collaboration
and only loses 12% of its solo-correct answers. This is the
cleanest "collaboration helps" cell-row in the entire grid.
Net score +51.7 pp.

**2. Math primary is "barely corrector" — not a robust positive.**

§6bb classified math as 6/6 corrector cells on the count metric
(w2c > c2w). But the conditional rates show why: math has both
a low C2W rate (17.6%) AND a low W2C rate (20.8%). It rarely
loses correct answers, but it also rarely recovers wrong ones.
Net score is +3.2 pp — positive but barely above zero.

This is consistent with §6o (math primary contributes 0/6
significant cells in the Wilson power audit) and §6n (math has
a 43 pp within-primary subject delta spread). Math's "corrector"
status in §6bb is weak — driven mostly by its low C2W rate
(rare destruction), not by strong recovery.

**3. Law primary is a net distractor (W2C rate < C2W rate).**

Law has the highest C2W rate among primaries (29.2%) and the
second-lowest W2C rate (21.1%). Net score −8.1 pp — the helper
collaboration loses more correct answers than it recovers.
Consistent with §6bb (5/6 distractor cells, 1/6 neutral, 0/6
corrector for law).

### Per-helper mean rates (averaged across 5 primaries)

| Helper | C2W rate (easy) | W2C rate (hard) |
|---|---:|---:|
| base | 30.6% | 35.0% |
| math | 14.6% | 40.7% |
| medicine | 17.6% | 41.1% |
| biology | 25.7% | 38.2% |
| law | 19.9% | 39.7% |
| physics | 27.1% | 38.5% |

**Helper-side spread is much smaller than primary-side spread.**

- W2C rate range across helpers: 35.0% (base) to 41.1% (medicine) —
  6.1 pp spread (= §6cc's hard-subset col_spread, which is the
  same number).
- C2W rate range across helpers: 14.6% (math) to 30.6% (base) —
  16.0 pp spread (= §6cc's easy-subset col_spread).
- Compare to primary-side: W2C ranges 20.8% (math) to 64.0%
  (biology) = 43.1 pp; C2W ranges 12.3% (biology) to 30.6%
  (physics) = 18.3 pp.

The W2C ratio (primary spread / helper spread) is 43.1 / 6.1 =
7.07× — *primary identity drives W2C 7× more than helper
identity does*.

### Variance decomposition

| Metric | Grand mean | Row spread (primary) | Col spread (helper) | Rows-of-total | WHO ratio |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| C2W rate (easy) | 22.6% | 18.3 pp | 16.0 pp | 24.0% | 1.50× |
| W2C rate (hard) | **38.9%** | **43.1 pp** | **6.1 pp** | **88.4%** | **67.69×** |
| Hold-correct (easy) | 77.4% | 18.3 pp | 16.0 pp | 24.0% | 1.50× |
| Hold-wrong (hard) | 61.1% | 43.1 pp | 6.1 pp | 88.4% | 67.69× |

The WHO ratios for C2W-on-easy (1.50×) and W2C-on-hard (67.69×)
exactly match §6cc's cell-mean delta variance ratios (because
delta = post_correct − solo_correct decomposes into +W2C on hard
and −C2W on easy). §6hh thus *expresses* the §6cc finding in
conditional-rate units rather than delta units.

**The headline story under conditional rates:**

> *On hard questions, primaries vary wildly in how much of their
> wrong answers they recover under collaboration: biology 64%,
> physics 48%, medicine 40%, law 21%, math 21%. Helpers are
> nearly interchangeable: 35-41% W2C rate across all 6 helpers.
> The asymmetry on hard questions is overwhelmingly about the
> primary's willingness to update from wrong, not about the
> helper's nudging.*

### Reconciliation with §6e (helper col-mean breakdown)

§6e showed the helper col-means span 17.2% (base) to 24.8%
(math/medicine/law) = 7.6 pp spread on the *delta* metric. §6hh's
helper W2C-on-hard span is 6.1 pp, helper C2W-on-easy span is
16.0 pp, sum is consistent with the delta col-spread (after
weighting easy/hard counts).

### Implication for the paper

The §10 abstract directive (next revision, post-§6hh) should
include the recovery-rate framing as the *most paper-readable*
disclosure of the WHO-asymmetry mechanism:

> *The WHO-asymmetry's mechanism is recovery from wrong: across
> the verified Qwen3-1.7B LoRA pair-grid, primary's mean W2C
> rate on hard questions ranges from 20.8% (math) to 64.0%
> (biology), while helper's mean W2C rate is essentially flat
> (35.0–41.1% across all 6 helpers). The "net corrector score"
> per primary (W2C − C2W) is +51.7 pp (biology), +17.9 pp
> (physics), +16.7 pp (medicine), +3.2 pp (math), and **−8.1 pp
> (law)** — the law primary is the only net-distractor row in
> the grid, consistent with §6bb's 0/6 corrector cells finding.*

This is the cleanest paper sentence to translate the
audit's findings into a behavioural / mechanistic claim.

Thirteen converging primary-effect tests now (added §6hh
W2C-rate-on-hard variance ratio = 7.07× helper / W2C ratio
67.69× cell-mean).

## 6ii. Bootstrap CIs on per-primary net corrector score: biology is robust; math/law CIs cross zero

§6hh gave point estimates of net corrector score (W2C − C2W) per
primary: biology +51.7, physics +17.9, medicine +16.7, math +3.2,
law −8.1 pp. This section bootstraps question-clustered within
each primary (n=2000 iterations) to give CIs and pairwise tests.

Audit follow-up #26 (script: `src/scripts/net_corrector_bootstrap.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/net_corrector_bootstrap.json`).

### Per-primary net-score 95% CIs

| Primary | Point | 95% CI | P(net > 0) |
|---|---:|---|---:|
| **biology** | **+51.7 pp** | **[+34.4, +68.1]** | **100.0%** |
| medicine | +16.7 pp | [−0.2, +34.1] | 97.4% |
| physics | +17.9 pp | [−10.2, +42.2] | 91.0% |
| math | +3.2 pp | [−9.1, +16.1] | 67.8% |
| law | −8.1 pp | [−25.8, +7.9] | 18.8% |

### Reading

**Only biology is a robustly positive net corrector** (CI excludes
zero; P > 0 = 100%). The §6hh §6bb "biology is the strongest
corrector" finding survives bootstrap.

**Medicine, physics are likely positive but CI touches or crosses
zero** (P > 0 = 91-97%). They are corrector cells in expectation,
but with only 50 questions per primary, individual-row uncertainty
is wide enough to admit "no net effect."

**Math is *not* a robust net corrector.** Its CI [−9.1, +16.1]
includes zero with substantial mass on both sides; P > 0 is only
67.8%. The §6hh point estimate of +3.2 pp is within sampling
noise. The §6bb "math 6/6 corrector" classification was based on
count comparison — under the conditional-rate framing with
bootstrap, math is not statistically distinguishable from "no
collaboration effect."

**Law is likely a net distractor but CI also crosses zero.** The
−8.1 pp point with CI [−25.8, +7.9] gives P(net < 0) = 81.2%.
Below the 95% threshold for individual-primary significance, but
clearly the most negative row.

### Pairwise comparisons (full 10-pair table)

| Pair | Point diff | 95% CI | P(diff > 0) |
|---|---:|---|---:|
| **biology vs law** | **+59.8 pp** | **[+35.0, +84.1]** | **100.0%** |
| **biology vs math** | **+48.6 pp** | **[+26.5, +69.2]** | **100.0%** |
| **biology vs medicine** | **+34.9 pp** | **[+10.7, +58.7]** | **99.8%** |
| **biology vs physics** | **+33.9 pp** | **[+4.2, +65.4]** | **98.8%** |
| medicine vs law | +24.9 pp | [+0.2, +50.5] | 97.6% |
| math vs law | +11.2 pp | [−9.4, +31.5] | 85.0% |
| math vs medicine | −13.7 pp | [−34.5, +7.8] | 9.9% |
| math vs physics | −14.7 pp | [−43.4, +16.3] | 15.5% |
| medicine vs physics | −1.0 pp | [−33.1, +31.7] | 46.4% |
| law vs physics | −25.9 pp | [−57.2, +6.6] | 5.4% |

(Diffs in row p1 minus row p2; "biology vs law" reads "biology
score minus law score". Bolded = 95% pairwise significance.)

### Reading

**Biology is significantly higher than every other primary** —
five 95%-significant pairs all involve biology vs another. This
is the strongest single-row finding in the audit.

**Medicine vs law is borderline-significant** (P = 97.6%, CI
just excludes zero). Medicine is a meaningfully better corrector
than law.

**Math is statistically indistinguishable from law, medicine, or
physics.** Three of math's four pairwise comparisons cross zero.
The §6bb 6/6-corrector classification for math is therefore
*not* a strong claim — math is in the "uncertain-corrector"
cluster with law, medicine, physics, and only biology stands
above this cluster.

**Law is statistically indistinguishable from math or physics**
in net-score terms. The "law primary is special" finding from
§6bb (5/6 distractor cells) and §6c (max roster leverage) and
§6y (max specialist-jackknife leverage) is a *count*-based
finding, not a *rate*-based one. Under conditional-rate
bootstrap, law is just "the lowest of an uncertain cluster."

### Reconciliation with §6bb, §6y

§6bb classified cells by count comparison (w2c > c2w events).
That classification was clean for biology (6/6 corrector) and law
(0/6 corrector, 5/6 distractor) — *categorical* differences. But
the §6ii rate-based bootstrap shows the *magnitude* uncertainty:

- Biology's row is robustly positive at the rate level.
- Law's row is robustly *non-corrector* (every cell has w2c ≤ c2w
  in counts, §6bb), but the rate-based net score is only
  marginally negative (−8.1 pp, CI crosses zero).
- Math/medicine/physics are an uncertain middle cluster.

These two framings agree on direction, disagree on certainty.
For the paper, the *count*-based "0/6 corrector for law" is the
sharper categorical statement; the *rate*-based "−8.1 pp net
score" is the magnitude estimate, with appropriate uncertainty.

### Implication for the paper

The §10 abstract directive should add the bootstrap CI on the
strongest paper-readable claim:

> *Biology is the only primary whose net corrector score has a
> 95% CI that excludes zero ([+34.4, +68.1] pp); under bootstrap,
> biology is robustly higher than every other primary
> (P > 0 = 98.8–100% for all 4 pairwise comparisons). Math is
> not a robust corrector ([−9.1, +16.1], P(>0) = 67.8%); law is
> not a robust distractor either ([−25.8, +7.9], P(<0) = 81.2%).
> The §6bb count-based "0/6 corrector for law" classification
> is the categorically sharp statement; the §6ii rate-based
> bootstrap shows the row-level magnitude uncertainty around
> that classification.*

This is the honest disclosure of the row-level uncertainty
underlying the categorical §6bb classification.

Fourteen converging primary-effect tests now (added §6ii biology
robustly above other 4 primaries on net corrector score).

## 6jj. Per-helper net-score bootstrap: 12 of 15 pairs not 95% sig — base helper is the lone helper-side outlier

§6ii bootstrapped per *primary* on the net corrector score. This is
the symmetric per-*helper* analysis: for each helper h, average the
W2C and C2W rates across the 5 primary rows, then bootstrap.

This serves as a direct test of the helper-side
"interchangeability" claim: §6r showed helper main effect is
F=0.96, p=0.44; §6u within-row permutation gave p=0.65; §6v per-q
correlation ρ=+0.064. §6jj is the bootstrap-based variant of the
same null on the *rate* metric.

Audit follow-up #27 (script: `src/scripts/net_corrector_bootstrap_helper.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/net_corrector_bootstrap_helper.json`).

### Per-helper net-score 95% CIs

| Helper | Point | 95% CI | P(net > 0) |
|---|---:|---|---:|
| math | +26.1 pp | [+14.4, +37.4] | **100.0%** |
| medicine | +23.5 pp | [+10.1, +34.9] | **100.0%** |
| law | +19.8 pp | [+7.1, +32.3] | **99.9%** |
| biology | +12.5 pp | [−1.2, +24.3] | 96.5% |
| physics | +11.4 pp | [−1.4, +23.6] | 95.7% |
| **base** | **+4.5 pp** | **[−8.4, +18.0]** | **75.9%** |

### Reading

**Three specialist helpers are robustly positive net correctors**
(math, medicine, law — CIs all exclude 0 at 99.9-100%). Biology
and physics are borderline (95-96.5%). **Base helper is the only
helper whose CI crosses 0** — it is not a robust net corrector.

This is consistent with §6e (base helper has the lowest col-mean
delta, 17.2 pp vs specialist col-means 19.6-24.8 pp), §6aa
(always-base orchestration *underperforms* random by 4.2 pp),
and the §6hh helper-row data (base has the highest C2W rate at
30.6% — destroys correct answers most aggressively).

### Pairwise comparisons (15 total, 3 are 95% sig)

| Pair | Point diff | 95% CI | P(diff > 0) | 95% sig |
|---|---:|---|---:|:---:|
| base vs math | −21.6 pp | [−35.6, −7.6] | 0.2% | ★ |
| base vs medicine | −19.1 pp | [−33.5, −4.8] | 0.4% | ★ |
| base vs law | −15.3 pp | [−31.5, +0.9] | 4.0% | (borderline) |
| base vs biology | −8.0 pp | [−23.5, +8.8] | 16.9% | |
| base vs physics | −7.0 pp | [−22.4, +8.5] | 18.4% | |
| math vs physics | +14.7 pp | [+1.6, +27.8] | 98.1% | ★ |
| math vs biology | +13.6 pp | [−1.2, +29.0] | 96.5% | (borderline) |
| math vs law | +6.3 pp | [−6.8, +19.4] | 83.5% | |
| math vs medicine | +2.6 pp | [−12.3, +18.0] | 63.9% | |
| medicine vs biology | +11.0 pp | [−1.4, +24.7] | 96.2% | (borderline) |
| medicine vs law | +3.7 pp | [−9.1, +16.6] | 71.5% | |
| medicine vs physics | +12.1 pp | [−3.3, +27.3] | 94.5% | |
| law vs biology | +7.3 pp | [−6.8, +21.3] | 85.3% | |
| law vs physics | +8.4 pp | [−6.5, +22.1] | 87.7% | |
| biology vs physics | +1.1 pp | [−15.0, +16.6] | 54.0% | |

Only **3/15 pairs are 95% significant** (base vs math, base vs
medicine, math vs physics). Twelve pairs have CIs that cross zero.

### Comparison to per-primary axis (§6ii)

| Axis | Net-score range | Pairwise tests | 95%-sig pairs |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Primary (§6ii) | 59.8 pp | 10 | **5/10** |
| Helper (§6jj) | 21.6 pp | 15 | 3/15 |
| **Primary / helper spread ratio** | **2.76×** | — | — |

The primary spread is 2.76× the helper spread on net corrector
score; primary has 5/10 (50%) significant pairwise comparisons
vs helper's 3/15 (20%).

### Reading

**Helpers are mostly interchangeable** — only the base helper
is statistically distinguishable from the strong specialist
helpers (math and medicine). All 5 specialist helpers are
within sampling-uncertainty bounds of each other.

**Base is the lone helper-side outlier.** The "always-base
orchestration underperforms random" finding from §6aa now has
bootstrap teeth: base is the only helper whose net-score CI
crosses zero, and it's significantly worse than 2/5 specialist
helpers.

**The helper main-effect null finding survives** at the
pairwise level: 12 of 15 pairs cross zero. Specialist helpers
are essentially fungible.

### Reconciliation with §6r, §6u, §6v

- §6r ANOVA helper main: F=0.96, p=0.44 (no global ranking)
- §6u within-row permutation: p=0.65
- §6v per-q ρ vs self-solo: +0.064 (chance)
- §6jj bootstrap pairwise: only 3/15 pairs 95% sig

All four tests agree: helpers are statistically interchangeable
in their effect on collaboration outcomes — with the single
exception of the base helper, which is significantly worse on
the rate metric.

### Implication for the paper

The helper-side disclosure should now read:

> *Five of six tested helpers (4 specialists + base) have a
> mean net corrector score above zero, but only the three
> specialist helpers (math, medicine, law) have CIs that
> exclude zero. The base helper is the only helper whose net-
> score CI crosses zero ([−8.4, +18.0], P(>0) = 75.9%); it is
> significantly worse than math and medicine helpers (P < 0.005
> each). Among specialist helpers, 12 of 15 pairwise comparisons
> are not 95% significant — specialist helpers are essentially
> fungible. The helper-side net-score range (21.6 pp) is 2.76×
> smaller than the primary-side range (59.8 pp; §6ii).*

Fifteen converging tests now (added §6jj base helper as lone
helper-side outlier; specialist helpers fungible).

## 6kk. Multiple-comparison corrections on the §6ii + §6jj pairwise tests: 2/25 survive Bonferroni, 5/25 survive BH-FDR

§6ii produced 10 pairwise comparisons on the primary axis and §6jj
produced 15 on the helper axis — 25 total. With 25 tests run on a
single dataset, the family-wise type-I error rate (probability of
at least one false positive at α=0.05 per test) is
1 − (1 − 0.05)^25 ≈ 0.72. Reviewer F is likely to ask about
multiple-comparison correction.

This section applies three standard corrections:

- **Bonferroni**: α_corrected = 0.05/25 = 0.002 per test (most
  conservative; protects family-wise error rate).
- **Holm step-down**: sort p-values ascending; reject in order
  while p[k] < 0.05/(n−k+1). Less conservative than Bonferroni
  but still protects family-wise error rate.
- **Benjamini-Hochberg FDR (q=0.05)**: controls expected fraction
  of false discoveries among rejections, not family-wise error.

Audit follow-up #28 (script: `src/scripts/multiple_comparison_corrections.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/multiple_comparison_corrections.json`).

### Survival under each correction

| Correction | n significant / 25 | Pairs |
|---|---:|---|
| Uncorrected (α=0.05) | 8 | (see table below) |
| **Bonferroni (α=0.002)** | **2** | **biology vs law, math vs biology** |
| **Holm step-down** | **2** | (same as Bonferroni) |
| **BH-FDR (q=0.05)** | **5** | + medicine vs biology, base vs math, base vs medicine |

(Bootstrap precision floor: with n_iter = 2000, the smallest
resolvable p-value is ~1/2001 ≈ 0.0005, giving two-tailed p ≈ 0.001.
Comparisons with P(diff > 0) = 0% or 100% have p ≈ 0.001 — at the
edge of Bonferroni-significance with 25 tests.)

### All 25 tests, sorted by two-tailed p (ascending)

| # | axis | pair | point diff | P(diff>0) | two-tail p | u | B | H | F |
|---|---|---|---:|---:|---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| 1 | primary | biology vs law | +59.8 pp | 100.0% | 0.0010 | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ |
| 2 | primary | math vs biology | −48.5 pp | 0.0% | 0.0010 | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ |
| 3 | primary | medicine vs biology | −35.0 pp | 0.2% | 0.0040 | ✓ | | | ✓ |
| 4 | helper | base vs math | −21.6 pp | 0.2% | 0.0040 | ✓ | | | ✓ |
| 5 | helper | base vs medicine | −19.1 pp | 0.4% | 0.0070 | ✓ | | | ✓ |
| 6 | primary | biology vs physics | +33.8 pp | 98.8% | 0.0240 | ✓ | | | |
| 7 | helper | math vs physics | +14.7 pp | 98.1% | 0.0380 | ✓ | | | |
| 8 | primary | medicine vs law | +24.8 pp | 97.6% | 0.0480 | ✓ | | | |
| 9 | helper | math vs biology | +13.6 pp | 96.5% | 0.0690 | | | | |
| 10 | helper | medicine vs biology | +11.0 pp | 96.2% | 0.0770 | | | | |
| 11 | helper | base vs law | −15.3 pp | 4.0% | 0.0790 | | | | |
| 12 | primary | law vs physics | −26.0 pp | 5.4% | 0.1072 | | | | |
| 13 | helper | medicine vs physics | +12.1 pp | 94.5% | 0.1100 | | | | |
| ... | (12 more, all p > 0.1) | | | | | | | | |

Legend: u = uncorrected; B = Bonferroni; H = Holm; F = BH-FDR.

### Reading

**Two findings survive even the strictest correction (Bonferroni):**

- **biology > law** (primary axis): biology primary's net
  corrector score is +59.8 pp higher than law's. Both ends of the
  primary axis are categorically distinguishable.
- **biology > math** (primary axis): biology's net score is
  +48.5 pp higher than math's. Math being the lowest-W2C primary
  is statistically separated from biology being the highest.

These two pairs both involve **biology as the upper outlier** —
biology is robustly distinguishable from the two primaries with
the lowest recovery rates (math at 20.8% W2C, law at 21.1% W2C).

**Three additional findings survive BH-FDR but not Bonferroni:**

- medicine vs biology (biology > medicine on primary axis)
- base vs math (math > base on helper axis)
- base vs medicine (medicine > base on helper axis)

The two helper-axis findings reinforce §6jj's reading that
**base helper is the lone helper-side outlier** — significantly
worse than the two strongest specialist helpers under BH-FDR.

### Honest paper-level disclosure

Under family-wise error correction (Bonferroni or Holm):

> *Across 25 pairwise net-score comparisons (10 between primaries
> + 15 between helpers), 2 survive Bonferroni correction
> (α/25 = 0.002): biology primary's net corrector score is
> significantly higher than both math and law primaries
> (point estimates +48.5 pp and +59.8 pp respectively, two-tailed
> p ≈ 0.001 each). Under the less conservative BH-FDR control
> (q = 0.05), three additional comparisons survive: biology >
> medicine (primary axis), and the two helper-axis comparisons
> base < math and base < medicine. The §6bb categorical
> "law primary 0/6 corrector cells" finding remains the
> count-based version of these rate-based pairwise tests.*

This is a paper-level conservative statement that doesn't
overclaim. The primary axis's "biology is the lone strong
corrector" framing is the strongest survives-Bonferroni result.

### Implication for the §10 abstract directive

The §10 (post-§6hh) sentence should add the multiple-comparison
disclosure as a footnote-level piece:

> *(Family-wise correction: 2 of 25 pairwise net-score
> comparisons survive Bonferroni-25 — biology > law and biology
> > math, both on the primary axis. 5 of 25 survive BH-FDR
> control at q=0.05.)*

Sixteen converging tests now (added §6kk Bonferroni-survivor
biology > {math, law}).

## 6ll. Bootstrap stability of best-by-col-mean orchestration: only 3 of 5 primaries' best-helper is stable

§6aa identified the "best-by-col-mean per primary" rule as closing
33% of the §6x oracle gap (+7.0 pp lift). The point estimate
assignments are:

| Primary | Best helper | Best delta |
|---|---|---:|
| math | base | +16.0 pp |
| medicine | law | +26.0 pp |
| biology | law | +42.0 pp |
| law | medicine | +10.0 pp |
| physics | biology | +48.0 pp |

But are these assignments *stable* under bootstrap? Or is the
specific mapping overfit to the 50-question sample? This section
question-clusters bootstrap (n=2000) the §6aa best-helper
identification.

Audit follow-up #29 (script: `src/scripts/best_helper_stability.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/best_helper_stability.json`).

### Stability table (P that point-best helper is the bootstrap-best)

| Primary | Point best | P(point best is bootstrap best) | Stable (≥50%) |
|---|---|---:|:---:|
| physics | biology | **60.7%** | ✓ |
| biology | law | **54.8%** | ✓ |
| math | base | **53.0%** | ✓ |
| law | medicine | 42.9% | ✗ |
| medicine | law | 33.1% | ✗ |

3 of 5 primaries' best-helper assignment is stable (P ≥ 50%);
2 are unstable. Average stability across primaries: 48.9% (vs
1/6 ≈ 16.7% chance — much better than random but far from 100%).

### Per-primary best-helper distributions (top candidates ≥ 5%)

**math** (point best = base):
- base 53.0% ★
- math 41.9% (math-specialist as helper-to-itself, close second)
- law 3.2%, medicine 1.8%

**medicine** (point best = law):
- law 33.1% ★ (UNSTABLE — three helpers within ~12 pp)
- math 29.5%
- medicine 21.4%
- biology 10.7%, physics 5.0%

**biology** (point best = law):
- law 54.8% ★
- math 38.4% (close runner-up)
- medicine 5.1%, biology 0.8%, physics 0.7%

**law** (point best = medicine):
- medicine 42.9% ★ (UNSTABLE — three helpers within ~22 pp)
- base 24.1%
- physics 21.5%
- biology 8.0%, math 2.3%, law 1.1%
