**physics** (point best = biology):
- biology 60.7% ★ (most stable assignment)
- medicine 21.6%
- math 13.5%
- law 2.6%, physics 1.7%

### Reading

**Three findings:**

1. **The §6aa "best-by-col-mean" rule is directionally robust but
   point-specific assignments are uncertain.** Three of five
   primaries' best-helper is stable; two are not.

2. **The "cross-domain helpers preferred" framing is conceptually
   robust** — even under bootstrap, the top-2 candidates per
   primary are dominated by cross-domain helpers (math is the
   only primary where base is the leading candidate; medicine
   and law primary's top candidates are cross-domain;
   biology→law/math; physics→biology/medicine).

3. **Medicine and law primaries have genuinely ambiguous best
   helpers** — multiple specialists are within sampling-noise
   distance of each other. For these primaries, the §6aa point
   estimate of "law" or "medicine" is not a robust selection.

### Reconciliation with §6aa, §6jj

§6aa reports the *expected gap-closure* (+7.0 pp) from picking
the point-estimate best helper. §6jj/§6ll show that:

- The selected helper varies across bootstrap iterations (3/5
  stable selections).
- Specialist helpers are mostly statistically interchangeable
  (§6jj: 12/15 helper pairs not significantly different).
- The *aggregate* gap-closure may be smaller in expectation
  than the in-sample +7.0 pp, since sometimes the picked
  helper is the second-best rather than the true best.

A LOO-style cross-validation on best-helper selection (queued
as audit follow-up #30) would directly estimate the
out-of-sample gap-closure.

### Implication for the paper

The §10 abstract directive should disclose orchestration
stability as a caveat:

> *Among non-learned orchestration predictors, the best-by-
> col-mean rule closes 33% of the +21.4 pp oracle gap (+7.0 pp
> lift in-sample) — but this is the in-sample lift. Under
> question-clustered bootstrap (n=2000), the per-primary
> best-helper assignment is stable (P ≥ 50%) for only 3 of 5
> primaries (physics→biology, biology→law, math→base);
> medicine and law primaries' best helpers are statistically
> ambiguous. The "cross-domain helpers preferred" finding
> remains directionally robust — for 4 of 5 primaries, the
> top two candidates under bootstrap are cross-domain
> specialists.*

This is the honest disclosure of orchestration uncertainty.

Seventeen converging primary-effect tests now (added §6ll
3/5 primary best-helper assignments stable under bootstrap).

## 6mm. LOO-CV on best-helper selection: in-sample +7.0 pp drops to +1.8 pp out-of-sample (overfitting penalty 5.2 pp)

§6aa reported "best-by-col-mean per primary" closes 33% of the
+21.4 pp oracle gap (+7.0 pp lift in-sample). §6ll showed only
3/5 primary best-helper assignments are stable under bootstrap.
This section directly tests out-of-sample generalization via
leave-one-question-out cross-validation.

Audit follow-up #30 (script: `src/scripts/best_helper_loo_cv.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/best_helper_loo_cv.json`).

### Method

For each (primary, idx) tuple in the 50-question set:

1. Hold out question idx.
2. On the remaining 49 questions, compute each helper's col-mean
   accuracy; identify the best-by-col-mean helper.
3. Apply the chosen helper's prediction on the held-out question
   idx.
4. Record whether the LOO prediction is correct.

Pool LOO accuracy across (5 × 50) = 250 LOO predictions.

### Results

| Strategy | Pooled accuracy | Lift vs random | Gap closed |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Random (mean across 6 helpers) | 51.0% | — | — |
| **In-sample best-by-col-mean (§6aa)** | **58.0%** | **+7.0 pp** | **33%** |
| **LOO-CV best-by-col-mean (§6mm)** | **52.8%** | **+1.8 pp** | **8%** |
| Oracle ceiling (best-of-6 per question) | 72.4% | +21.4 pp | 100% |

**Overfitting penalty: in-sample 58.0% → LOO-CV 52.8% = −5.2 pp.**

The §6aa "33% gap closure" reduces to **8% gap closure** under
honest out-of-sample evaluation. The orchestration rule is
*directionally* useful but the in-sample lift was substantially
inflated.

### Per-primary LOO-CV breakdown

| Primary | LOO acc | Point best helper | LOO-picks point-best |
|---|---:|---|---:|
| biology | 74.0% | law | 94.0% |
| physics | 60.0% | biology | 100.0% |
| medicine | 46.0% | law | 90.0% |
| math | 42.0% | base | 90.0% |
| law | 42.0% | medicine | 100.0% |

The picked helper matches the in-sample best 90-100% of the time
across all primaries — the rule is *highly stable in terms of
which helper it picks*. But the picked helper's actual accuracy
on held-out questions is lower than its in-sample col-mean
suggests. The +5.2 pp overfitting penalty is dominated by the
math, medicine, and law primaries' LOO accuracies (42-46%) being
much lower than their in-sample col-means (52-62%).

### Why does the rule overfit?

Two mechanisms:

1. **Small-N noise on the picked helper's mean.** Each cell's
   accuracy estimate uses only 50 questions; the col-mean has
   ~7% sampling SD. Picking the empirical maximum among 6
   noisy estimates introduces upward bias on the picked
   helper's expected accuracy (regression to the mean on the
   held-out sample).

2. **Selection bias on which helper is picked when removing a
   particular question.** A question that the in-sample best
   gets right doesn't change the ranking under LOO; a question
   it gets wrong might. The held-out question is therefore
   *inversely correlated* with the picked helper's accuracy on
   that question (the second-best helper is picked precisely
   when removing the held-out question makes the in-sample
   best's accuracy look worse).

Both effects shrink the LOO accuracy below the in-sample
col-mean.

### Reconciliation with §6aa, §6ll, §6jj

| Section | Claim | Status under §6mm |
|---|---|---|
| §6aa | "Best-by-col-mean closes 33% of oracle gap" | Reduced to 8% under LOO-CV |
| §6aa | "Cross-domain pairing preferred 4/5 primaries" | Still robust (LOO-CV picks cross-domain at 90-100% per primary) |
| §6ll | "3/5 primary best-helpers stable under bootstrap" | LOO confirms: 3/5 primaries' LOO accuracy holds the in-sample lift signal |
| §6jj | "12/15 helper pairs not significantly different" | Consistent: when in-sample picks are noisy, LOO has high regret |

The §6aa orchestration framing is **directionally robust but
quantitatively overstated**. The honest paper-readable claim
is:

> *Out-of-sample evaluation (LOO-CV) of the best-by-col-mean
> orchestration rule closes only 8% of the oracle gap (+1.8 pp
> over random helper choice), compared to the in-sample 33%
> claim from §6aa. The overfitting penalty is 5.2 pp,
> consistent with the §6ll finding that the per-primary
> best-helper assignment is bootstrap-stable for only 3/5
> primaries. Cross-domain pairing remains directionally
> robust — LOO picks match the cross-domain in-sample
> assignment at 90-100% rate per primary — but the absolute
> gap closure under honest evaluation is much smaller than
> in-sample.*

### Implication for the paper

The §10 abstract directive should report the LOO-CV gap
closure (8%) as the headline orchestration claim, with
in-sample 33% as a "compare to" footnote. Specifically:

> *Out-of-sample (LOO-CV), the best-by-col-mean orchestration
> rule closes 8% of a +21.4 pp oracle gap (+1.8 pp lift over
> random helper choice), compared to 33% in-sample (+7.0 pp).
> The overfitting penalty (5.2 pp) reflects the small-N
> sampling noise on per-cell accuracy estimates with N=50
> per cell. Cross-domain helpers remain preferred over
> domain-matched helpers under LOO-CV: the picked helper is
> cross-domain for 4 of 5 primaries (the only exception is
> math primary's base helper).*

This is the most important single revision to the §6aa
orchestration framing — the in-sample claim was substantially
inflated.

Eighteen converging primary-effect tests now (added §6mm
overfitting penalty 5.2 pp / LOO-CV gap 8%).

## 6nn. Difficulty-stratified LOO-CV: orchestration helps on easy questions (+6.4 pp), is essentially zero on hard (+0.9 pp)

§6mm computed LOO-CV on the full 50-question set per primary,
finding +1.8 pp lift (8% of oracle gap closed). This section
restricts the LOO to easy or hard subsets separately, asking
whether orchestration generalizes better where the WHO-asymmetry
is largest (hard subset, §6cc 67.69× ratio).

Audit follow-up #31 (script: `src/scripts/best_helper_loo_difficulty.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/best_helper_loo_difficulty.json`).

### Subset-level results

| Subset | n | Random | LOO-CV | In-sample | Oracle | LOO lift | LOO gap closed | Overfit penalty |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Easy (solo correct) | 74 | 77.4% | **83.8%** | 91.9% | 98.6% | **+6.4 pp** | **30%** | 8.1 pp |
| Hard (solo wrong) | 176 | 38.9% | **39.8%** | 46.0% | 61.4% | **+0.9 pp** | **4%** | 6.2 pp |
| All (pooled) | 250 | 51.0% | 52.8% | 58.0% | 72.4% | +1.8 pp | 8% | 5.2 pp |

### Reading

**Counterintuitive finding: orchestration LOO-CV gap-closure is
LARGER on easy questions (30%) than on hard (4%).**

Naively, hard questions should benefit *more* from orchestration —
they have a +22.5 pp oracle gap (61.4% − 38.9%) vs easy's +21.2 pp
gap (98.6% − 77.4%). And the §6cc/§6dd/§6ee analyses showed the
WHO-asymmetry concentrates on hard. Yet under honest LOO-CV,
hard-question orchestration realizes only **+0.9 pp lift, 4% of
gap** — barely above noise.

**Why?**

1. **Hard questions are *intrinsically* hard.** When the primary
   is solo-wrong, recovery requires the helper to provide
   *correct* additional information. But helpers' own knowledge
   on the question is noisy at the per-question level (§6v
   ρ=+0.064 between helper correctness and helper self-solo).
   Best-by-col-mean only identifies *aggregate* helper effectiveness;
   per-question recovery requires per-question helper-knowledge
   coupling that the rule can't capture.

2. **Easy questions are "easy" because solo is correct.** The
   helper's job there is to *not destroy* the correct answer
   (low C2W rate). The best-by-col-mean rule on easy correctly
   identifies the helper that least often destroys easy answers
   (typically math or medicine specialists with low C2W rate
   per §6hh: math 14.6%, medicine 17.6%). This is a *predictable*
   helper-side property.

3. **The "WHO-asymmetry on hard" is a primary-level finding,
   not a helper-orchestration opportunity.** §6cc showed primary
   identity explains 88.4% of cell-mean variance on hard. But
   that's a between-primary claim, not a within-primary helper
   choice. Within a primary's hard subset, helpers are mostly
   indistinguishable.

### Per-primary LOO accuracy by subset

| Primary | All (n=50) | Easy | Hard |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| biology | 74.0% | **94.7%** (n=19) | **61.3%** (n=31) |
| physics | 60.0% | 83.3% (n=6) | 56.8% (n=44) |
| medicine | 46.0% | 73.3% (n=15) | 34.3% (n=35) |
| math | 42.0% | 83.3% (n=18) | **18.8%** (n=32) |
| law | 42.0% | 81.2% (n=16) | **23.5%** (n=34) |

**Math primary's hard-question LOO accuracy is just 18.8%.**
Out of 32 hard math questions, only 6 are answered correctly under
the picked helper. The picked helper (base) doesn't help on math
primary's hard questions — math primary essentially can't recover.

**Law's hard accuracy is 23.5%** — second-lowest, consistent with
§6bb (5/6 distractor cells) and §6hh (W2C 21.1%).

**Biology's hard accuracy is 61.3%** — biology primary recovers
most of its hard questions even under LOO-CV, consistent with
§6hh W2C 64.0% (the highest per-primary W2C rate).

### Reconciliation with §6mm, §6cc

§6mm reported 8% gap closure pooled. §6nn shows this is a
weighted average of:
- Easy subset: 30% gap closure (orchestration works)
- Hard subset: 4% gap closure (orchestration nearly fails)

The pooled lift is dominated by the larger hard subset (n=176 vs
n=74 easy), so the 8% pooled is closer to hard than easy.

§6cc showed primary identity explains 88.4% of cell-mean variance
on hard. §6nn shows this *primary-level* asymmetry doesn't
translate into a *helper-level* orchestration opportunity:
within a primary's hard subset, picking a different helper
provides only +0.9 pp lift out-of-sample.

### Implication for the paper

The §10 abstract directive should refine the §6mm orchestration
disclosure with the subset breakdown:

> *Out-of-sample (LOO-CV), the best-by-col-mean orchestration
> rule closes 30% of the easy-question oracle gap (+6.4 pp lift
> on n=74 questions where the primary is solo-correct) but only
> 4% of the hard-question oracle gap (+0.9 pp lift on n=176
> questions where the primary is solo-wrong). The pooled +1.8 pp
> lift is a weighted average. Hard-question orchestration is
> essentially absent under honest evaluation: the §6cc
> WHO-asymmetry on hard (67.69× cell-mean ratio) is a
> primary-level effect — between-primary differences in
> recovery rate — not a helper-level orchestration opportunity.
> Best-by-col-mean works on easy questions (helper choice
> determines whether correct answers are preserved); it does
> not work on hard questions (where recovery requires
> per-question helper-knowledge coupling that the aggregate
> rule cannot capture).*

This is the most refined orchestration claim the audit can
support.

Nineteen converging primary-effect tests now (added §6nn
hard-LOO 4% gap closure / easy-LOO 30%).

## 6oo. Per-cell net corrector bootstrap CI: 8 of 30 cells robustly positive, 0 robustly negative

§6bb categorically classified 30 cells as corrector / distractor /
neutral on raw counts (24 / 5 / 1). §6ii bootstrapped per primary
(across helpers); §6jj bootstrapped per helper (across primaries).
This section is the most granular bootstrap: per individual cell,
question-clustered, with 95% CI on the net corrector score.

For each (primary, helper) cell, n=2000 bootstrap iterations:
resample 50 questions per primary with replacement; compute the
cell's W2C rate (recovery from solo-wrong) and C2W rate
(destruction of solo-correct); compute net = W2C − C2W.

Audit follow-up #32 (script: `src/scripts/per_cell_net_corrector_ci.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/per_cell_net_corrector_ci.json`).

### Per-cell significance class

| Class | Count | Pct |
|---|---:|---:|
| **CI excludes 0 (positive — robust corrector)** | **8/30** | **27%** |
| CI excludes 0 (negative — robust distractor) | 0/30 | 0% |
| CI crosses 0 (uncertain) | 22/30 | 73% |

**No cell is robustly distractor.** The §6bb categorical "5
distractor cells in law primary's row" finding is direction-
correct (point estimates negative) but **does not survive bootstrap
at the per-cell level** — every law-primary cell's 95% CI crosses
zero. The most-negative cell (physics × base, point −32.6 pp) has
CI [−71.7, +13.4].

### The 8 robust corrector cells

| Rank | Primary | Helper | Net point | 95% CI |
|---:|---|---|---:|---|
| 1 | biology | law | **+67.7 pp** | [+50.0, +84.4] |
| 2 | biology | math | +62.5 pp | [+42.5, +81.2] |
| 3 | biology | medicine | +52.0 pp | [+27.6, +74.6] |
| 4 | biology | base | +44.3 pp | [+21.0, +65.6] |
| 5 | biology | physics | +43.5 pp | [+17.4, +67.3] |
| 6 | biology | biology | +40.2 pp | [+15.4, +64.7] |
| 7 | medicine | law | +33.3 pp | [+11.5, +52.8] |
| 8 | math | math | +22.6 pp | [+3.3, +40.5] |

**The biology primary's *entire row* (all 6 cells) is robustly
positive.** This is the strongest single-row finding in the audit:
no helper, on biology primary, fails to bootstrap-significantly
move the cell toward correct answers.

The 2 non-biology robust cells: medicine paired with law specialist
(+33.3 pp, the largest cross-domain pair effect on medicine), and
math paired with math specialist (+22.6 pp, only self-match cell
that's robustly positive).

### Per-primary breakdown

| Primary | n_pos / 6 | n_neg / 6 | n_zero / 6 | Mean net (pt) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| biology | **6** | 0 | 0 | +51.7 pp |
| medicine | 1 | 0 | 5 | +16.7 pp |
| math | 1 | 0 | 5 | +3.2 pp |
| physics | 0 | 0 | 6 | +17.9 pp |
| law | 0 | 0 | 6 | −8.1 pp |

**Only biology has any cell-level robust positives in all 6 helper
positions.** Medicine and math each have one robust cell; law and
physics have none.

### Per-helper breakdown

| Helper | n_pos / 5 | n_neg / 5 | n_zero / 5 | Mean net (pt) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| math | 2 | 0 | 3 | +26.1 pp |
| law | 2 | 0 | 3 | +19.8 pp |
| medicine | 1 | 0 | 4 | +23.5 pp |
| biology | 1 | 0 | 4 | +12.5 pp |
| physics | 1 | 0 | 4 | +11.4 pp |
| base | 1 | 0 | 4 | +4.5 pp |

Math and law helpers contribute 4 of the 8 robust corrector cells
(biology paired with each, plus medicine × law and math × math).

### Reconciliation with §6bb, §6ii, §6jj, §6kk

| Section | Lens | Robust claim |
|---|---|---|
| §6bb | Cell counts (point) | 24/30 corrector, 5/30 distractor (in law row) |
| §6ii | Per-primary bootstrap | Biology only primary CI excluding 0 (P=100%) |
| §6jj | Per-helper bootstrap | Base only helper CI crossing 0; specialists fungible |
| §6kk | Bonferroni-25 | biology > {math, law} survive |
| §6oo | Per-cell bootstrap | 8/30 cells robust positive; 0 robust negative |

The §6oo result tightens §6bb: the categorical "5 distractor cells"
claim becomes "5 cells with negative point estimate but CI crossing
zero — directionally distractor but not bootstrap-firm at the cell
level." Aggregate-level (§6ii primary axis) is robust; individual
cell level (§6oo) is mostly not.

### Implication for the paper

The strongest single-cell-level claim the paper can make:

> *Per-cell bootstrap CIs on the net corrector score (W2C − C2W)
> show 8 of 30 cells are robustly positive (CI > 0): all 6 biology-
> primary cells (mean +51.7 pp), plus medicine × law (+33.3 pp) and
> math × math (+22.6 pp). No cell is robustly distractor under
> bootstrap. The §6bb count-based "5 distractor cells in law
> primary row" classification is direction-correct but does not
> survive cell-level CI testing — every law-row cell has a CI that
> crosses zero.*

This is the most cell-precise disclosure possible.

Twenty converging primary-effect tests now (added §6oo: biology
row 6/6 robust positive cells; only biology-primary row achieves
this).

## 6pp. Helper agreement on hard questions: 47.7% of hard questions are mutually unrecoverable — explains why orchestration fails on hard

§6nn showed orchestration LOO-CV closes only 4% of the
hard-question oracle gap. Why? This section provides the
mechanistic answer: a large fraction of hard questions are
*not recoverable by any helper*, so no orchestration rule can
help them.

For each hard question (primary solo wrong), count how many of
the 6 helper conditions led to a correct post-collab answer.
Bin questions by recovery count k ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6}:

- **k=0**: zero helpers recover (mutually unrecoverable —
  primary stays wrong with all 6 helpers).
- **k=6**: all 6 helpers recover (universally recoverable —
  primary always recovers).

Audit follow-up #33 (script: `src/scripts/helper_agreement_hard.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/helper_agreement_hard.json`).

### Pooled distribution (n=176 hard questions)

| k = # helpers that recover | Count | Pct |
|---:|---:|---:|
| **0/6 (mutually unrecoverable)** | **68** | **38.6%** |
| 1/6 | 16 | 9.1% |
| 2/6 | 17 | 9.7% |
| 3/6 | 11 | 6.2% |
| 4/6 | 18 | 10.2% |
| 5/6 | 17 | 9.7% |
| **6/6 (universally recoverable)** | **29** | **16.5%** |

Aggregating:
- **47.7% nearly-unrecoverable (0-1 of 6 helpers)** — orchestration cannot save these.
- 25% recoverable by minority of helpers (1-3 of 6) — orchestration *might* help if you pick the right one.
- 19.9% recoverable by majority (4-5 of 6).
- 16.5% universally recoverable (6/6).
- **36.4% recoverable by majority of helpers (4+ of 6).**

### Per-primary recovery distribution

| Primary | n_hard | 0/6 frac | 6/6 frac | mean recovery |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| **math** | 32 | **53.1%** | 3.1% | 1.25 / 6 (21%) |
| **law** | 34 | **52.9%** | 2.9% | 1.26 / 6 (21%) |
| medicine | 35 | 34.3% | 11.4% | 2.40 / 6 (40%) |
| physics | 44 | 34.1% | 22.7% | 2.91 / 6 (49%) |
| **biology** | 31 | **19.4%** | **41.9%** | 3.84 / 6 (64%) |

(The mean recovery / 6 per primary equals the §6hh per-primary
W2C rate — confirms internal consistency of the analysis.)

### Three findings

**1. Math and law primaries: >50% of hard questions are
mutually unrecoverable.** Math 17/32 (53%) and law 18/34 (53%)
of hard questions get *none* of 6 helpers to recover them. These
are essentially unhelpable by any orchestration rule.

**2. Biology primary: only 19.4% mutually unrecoverable; 41.9%
universally recoverable.** Biology hard questions are
structurally easier to recover — when biology gets a question
wrong solo, 6 of 6 helpers usually rescue it.

**3. The §6cc WHO-asymmetry on hard (67.69× ratio) is now
mechanistically explained: it's a primary-level difference in
*recoverability* of hard questions, not a helper-effect.**

Math primary's 53% mutually-unrecoverable rate vs biology's 19%
gives a 2.7× ratio. When you weight by mean recovery rate
(21% vs 64%), the difference is 3.05×. These are the underlying
quantities that drive the cell-mean WHO ratio.

### Reconciliation with §6nn, §6cc, §6hh

| Section | Finding | §6pp clarification |
|---|---|---|
| §6cc | Hard WHO ratio 67.69× | Driven by primary-level recoverability difference (53% vs 19% mutually unrecoverable) |
| §6hh | Per-primary W2C ranges 21-64% | Equals mean recovery / 6 per §6pp |
| §6nn | Hard LOO-CV gap 4% | Explained: ~48% of hard questions are nearly-unrecoverable, no helper choice helps them |
| §6oo | 0/30 cells robustly distractor | Explained: distractor "cells" are really mutually-unrecoverable questions, not "the helper made it worse" |

### Implication for the paper

The §10 abstract directive should add the recoverability
disclosure as the mechanistic explanation:

> *Of the 176 hard-question observations (primary solo wrong),
> **38.6% are recoverable by zero of 6 helpers** ("mutually
> unrecoverable" — primary stays wrong under any helper) and
> **47.7% are recoverable by ≤1 of 6 helpers** ("nearly
> unrecoverable"). The hard-question primary effect is driven by
> primary-level differences in mutually-unrecoverable rate:
> math and law primaries have 53% of their hard questions
> mutually unrecoverable, vs biology at 19% (and 42% universally
> recoverable). This is the mechanistic reason why orchestration
> closes only 4% of the hard-question oracle gap (§6nn) — for
> the bulk of hard questions, no helper choice helps.*

This is the cleanest single-sentence mechanism explanation in
the audit.

Twenty-one converging primary-effect tests now (added §6pp:
mutually-unrecoverable rate is the underlying primary difference
that drives the §6cc WHO-asymmetry on hard).

## 6qq. Subject decomposition of mutually-unrecoverable hard questions: math's 53% is concentrated in high_school_mathematics (75% unrec), elementary_math is only 20%

§6pp identified that 47.7% of hard questions are nearly-unrecoverable
(0–1 of 6 helpers help) and that math/law primaries have ~53%
mutually-unrecoverable rates vs biology's 19%. This section asks
whether that primary-level finding is uniformly distributed across
the primary's MMLU subjects, or concentrated in particular subjects.

If the latter, then the §6cc 67.69× hard WHO-asymmetry is partly
"which subjects MMLU treats as hard within the primary's domain"
rather than purely "primary-identity differences". This is the
direct response to Reviewer I's subject-mix concern restricted to
the hard subset.

Audit follow-up #34 (script: `src/scripts/mutually_unrecoverable_subjects.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/mutually_unrecoverable_subjects.json`).

### Per-primary breakdown by subject

For each (primary, subject) cell, n_hard = solo-wrong count, unrec =
mutually-unrecoverable count (0/6 helpers help), univ = universally-
recoverable count (6/6 help):

| Primary | Subject | n_total | n_hard | unrec | univ | unrec/hard | mean rec/6 |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| **math** | **high_school_mathematics** | 14 | 12 | **9** | 0 | **75.0%** | 0.58 |
| **math** | **college_mathematics** | 6 | 4 | **3** | 0 | **75.0%** | 0.25 |
| math | abstract_algebra | 8 | 6 | 3 | 0 | 50.0% | 1.50 |
| math | elementary_mathematics | 22 | 10 | 2 | 1 | 20.0% | 2.30 |
| medicine | virology | 7 | 5 | 3 | 1 | 60.0% | 2.00 |
| medicine | anatomy | 7 | 6 | 3 | 0 | 50.0% | 1.67 |
| medicine | medical_genetics | 6 | 4 | 2 | 1 | 50.0% | 1.75 |
| medicine | clinical_knowledge | 12 | 9 | 3 | 1 | 33.3% | 2.33 |
| medicine | professional_medicine | 13 | 7 | 1 | 1 | 14.3% | 3.43 |
| medicine | college_medicine | 5 | 4 | 0 | 0 | 0.0% | 3.00 |
| biology | college_biology | 13 | 9 | 3 | 4 | 33.3% | 3.44 |
| biology | high_school_biology | 37 | 22 | 3 | 9 | 13.6% | 4.00 |
| **law** | **professional_law** | 44 | 32 | **18** | 0 | **56.2%** | 1.12 |
| law | jurisprudence | 4 | 2 | 0 | 1 | 0.0% | 3.50 |
| physics | conceptual_physics | 18 | 16 | 7 | 3 | 43.8% | 2.38 |
| physics | high_school_physics | 15 | 14 | 4 | 3 | 28.6% | 3.07 |
| physics | college_physics | 7 | 7 | 2 | 2 | 28.6% | 3.00 |
| physics | astronomy | 10 | 7 | 2 | 2 | 28.6% | 3.71 |

### Top-15 subjects globally by mutual-unrecoverability rate (n_hard ≥ 3)

| Subject | n_hard | unrec | unrec/hard | mean rec/6 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| **high_school_mathematics** | 12 | 9 | **75.0%** | 0.58 |
| **college_mathematics** | 4 | 3 | **75.0%** | 0.25 |
| virology | 5 | 3 | 60.0% | 2.00 |
| **professional_law** | 32 | 18 | **56.2%** | 1.12 |
| abstract_algebra | 6 | 3 | 50.0% | 1.50 |
| anatomy | 6 | 3 | 50.0% | 1.67 |
| medical_genetics | 4 | 2 | 50.0% | 1.75 |
| conceptual_physics | 16 | 7 | 43.8% | 2.38 |
| clinical_knowledge | 9 | 3 | 33.3% | 2.33 |
| college_biology | 9 | 3 | 33.3% | 3.44 |
| high_school_physics | 14 | 4 | 28.6% | 3.07 |
| college_physics | 7 | 2 | 28.6% | 3.00 |
| astronomy | 7 | 2 | 28.6% | 3.71 |
| elementary_mathematics | 10 | 2 | 20.0% | 2.30 |
| professional_medicine | 7 | 1 | 14.3% | 3.43 |

Most recoverable (≥3 hard, lowest unrec rate): **high_school_biology**
13.6% (3/22), college_medicine 0% (0/4), professional_medicine 14.3%
(1/7), elementary_mathematics 20% (2/10).

### Five findings

**1. Math primary's 53% mutual-unrecoverability is dominated by
high_school_mathematics + college_mathematics, not abstract_algebra
or elementary_math.** Of 17 mutually-unrecoverable hard questions
in math primary, 9 are high_school_mathematics (75% of its hard) +
3 are college_mathematics (75%) + 3 are abstract_algebra (50%).
elementary_mathematics — math primary's largest subject (22 of 50)
— contributes only 2 mutually-unrecoverable questions (20% of its
n_hard). The math primary's "53% mutually unrecoverable" is
**not uniformly distributed** across its subjects.

**2. Law primary's 53% is essentially monolithic:
professional_law.** 32 of 34 law-primary hard questions are
professional_law; 18 of those are mutually unrecoverable (56.2%).
The law primary's mutual-unrecoverability finding is a
professional_law finding. Jurisprudence (the only other law
subject) contributes 0 of 18 unrec from 2 hard questions.

**3. Biology primary's low 19% is a high_school_biology finding.**
22 of 31 biology-primary hard questions are high_school_biology; 3
are mutually unrecoverable (13.6%) and 9 are universally recoverable
(40.9%). College_biology is more difficult (33.3% unrec) but smaller
(9 hard). Biology's recoverability advantage is partly that its
question pool is heavily weighted toward high_school_biology.

**4. Cross-primary subject hierarchy is consistent.**
high_school_mathematics (75%) > professional_law (56%) >
conceptual_physics (44%) > high_school_physics (29%) >
elementary_mathematics (20%) > high_school_biology (14%). The
four primaries with high mutual-unrecoverability are the four
where the largest subject in their pool is *also* high-unrec; the
single primary with low mutual-unrecoverability (biology) is the
one where the largest subject is high_school_biology (the most
recoverable subject).

**5. The §6cc 67.69× hard WHO-asymmetry is a confound between
primary identity and subject distribution** (this finding was
tentative, and §6ss empirically tests it). A math specialist
asked about high_school_mathematics is in a fundamentally harder
recovery regime than a biology specialist asked about
high_school_biology. The §6qq within-primary subject heterogeneity
(math: 20% to 75%; medicine: 0% to 60%; physics: 29% to 44%) is
large at the per-cell mutual-unrecoverability level. Whether
this dilutes the variance-decomposition headline is the question
addressed empirically in §6ss below — the answer is **no**, the
hard-subset subject/helper ratio (56.5×) actually *exceeds* the
hard primary/helper ratio (50.3×). The primary-identity
attribution does survive subject-stratification at the variance
level.

### Reconciliation with §6n, §6pp, §6cc

| Section | Finding | §6qq clarification |
|---|---|---|
| §6n | Within-primary subject delta spread: math 43 pp, law 43 pp, ≈ between-primary spread 34 pp | Confirmed at the recoverability level: math primary's unrec rate spans 20–75% across its 4 subjects |
| §6pp | Math primary 53% mutually-unrecoverable (17 of 32 hard) | Math's 17 unrec are not evenly distributed: 9 high_school_math, 3 college_math, 3 abstract_algebra, 2 elementary_math |
| §6cc | Hard WHO-asymmetry 67.69× | Driven jointly by (a) primary-identity differences in recovery, AND (b) primary-specific question-pool subject mix — cannot separate without a subject-balanced sampler |

### Implication for the paper (revised post-§6ss)

The §6qq finding identifies a real per-cell subject-mix structure:
within math primary, mutual-unrecoverability ranges from 20%
(elementary_mathematics) to 75% (high_school_mathematics +
college_mathematics). Within law primary, it is 56% on
professional_law (32 of 34 hard) and 0% on jurisprudence (n=2).
Biology primary's lower 19% rate is partly explained by its pool
being weighted toward high_school_biology (n=37 of 50, 14%
mutually unrecoverable).

This per-cell heterogeneity does NOT, however, dilute the
variance-decomposition headline. §6ss empirically replaces the
primary factor with the subject factor in the hard-subset ANOVA
and shows the subject/helper ratio actually *strengthens*
(50.3× → 56.5×). The primary-identity attribution survives
subject-stratification cleanly.

The honest paper-ready disclosure pairs §6qq with §6ss:

> *Within-primary mutual-unrecoverability heterogeneity is real
> at the per-cell level (math 20–75%, medicine 0–60%) and reflects
> the underlying subject-difficulty hierarchy (high_school_mathematics
> 75%, professional_law 56%, high_school_biology 14%). However, when
> the variance decomposition is recomputed with subject identity
> (17 levels) replacing primary identity (5 levels) as the row
> factor, the row/helper ratio is preserved (full grid: 21.7× ≈
> 22.1×; hard subset: 56.5× ≥ 50.3×). The "row factor dominates
> helper factor" finding is robust to the choice of row factor.
> Helper variance shrinks under hard-subset stratification (from
> 3.3% on full grid to 1.4% on hard).*

Twenty-two converging primary-effect tests after §6qq, twenty-three
after §6rr, twenty-four after §6ss — see the §6ss section below
for the formal subject-stratified test.

## 6rr. Subject-stratified WHO ratio: replacing primary (5 levels) with subject (17 levels) gives 21.7× ratio — virtually identical to primary's 22.1×

§6qq raised the subject-mix confound: within-primary subject
heterogeneity (math 20–75% mutual-unrec, medicine 0–60%) matches
between-primary spread. This section runs the formal test: replace
the §6m primary-identity factor (5 levels) with subject-identity
(19 MMLU subjects, 17 after filtering to n≥5) in the two-way
ANOVA on the cell-mean delta matrix, and compare variance
decompositions.

Audit follow-up #35 (script: `src/scripts/subject_who_ratio.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/subject_who_ratio.json`).

### Setup

For each (primary, subject) row, compute the per-cell delta against
each of 6 helpers as `mean(pair_correct[idx in subject]) − mean(solo_correct[idx in subject])`.
This gives a 17×6 (subject, n≥5) cell-mean matrix. Same two-way
ANOVA decomposition as §6m (n-weighted, additive model).

### Variance decomposition comparison

| Decomposition | row factor | levels | SS_row | SS_helper | SS_inter | row/helper ratio |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| **§6m (original)** | primary | 5 | **83.3%** | 3.8% | 12.9% | **22.11×** |
| **§6rr (full grid, uniform)** | subject | 19 | 65.2% | 3.3% | 31.5% | 19.96× |
| **§6rr (full grid, n-weighted)** | subject | 19 | 71.8% | 2.7% | 25.5% | 26.30× |
| **§6rr (n≥5, n-weighted)** | subject | 17 | **72.1%** | 3.3% | 24.6% | **21.73×** |

**The WHO-asymmetry ratio is essentially identical under either framing
(22.11× primary vs 21.73× subject).** Helper variance is ~3% under
both framings; the row factor (whether primary or subject) absorbs
72–83% of variance. Interaction is somewhat larger under subject
(24.6%) than under primary (12.9%) because individual subjects within
a primary have heterogeneous helper-interaction patterns.

### Cohen's f: subject 1.61 vs primary 2.24

Effect-size attenuation:
- §6m (primary):  Cohen's f = **2.24** (huge), helper f = 0.20 (small)
- §6rr (subject): Cohen's f = **1.61** (still huge), helper f = 0.19 (small)

Subject's f is smaller than primary's because the subject decomposition
spreads variance across more levels (17 vs 5), but it's still in the
"huge effect size" regime (Cohen's threshold for "large" is f ≥ 0.40).
The helper effect remains "small" under both decompositions.

### Filtered subject row means (cell-mean delta, n-weighted across 6 helpers)

Within-primary subject heterogeneity, sorted by primary then row mean:

| Primary | Subject | n | Row mean (Δ pp) |
|---|---|---:|---:|
| math | college_mathematics | 6 | **−5.6** |
| math | high_school_mathematics | 14 | +7.1 |
| math | abstract_algebra | 8 | +8.3 |
| math | elementary_mathematics | 22 | +9.8 |
| medicine | medical_genetics | 6 | +11.1 |
| medicine | virology | 7 | +19.0 |
| medicine | anatomy | 7 | +19.0 |
| medicine | professional_medicine | 13 | +21.8 |
| medicine | clinical_knowledge | 12 | +22.2 |
| medicine | college_medicine | 5 | +33.3 |
| **biology** | college_biology | 13 | +34.6 |
| **biology** | high_school_biology | 37 | +35.1 |
| **law** | professional_law | 44 | **+4.5** |
| physics | astronomy | 10 | +28.3 |
| physics | conceptual_physics | 18 | +34.3 |
| physics | high_school_physics | 15 | +46.7 |
| physics | college_physics | 7 | +50.0 |

(Excluded n<5: international_law n=2, jurisprudence n=4.)

### Five findings

**1. The WHO-asymmetry headline survives subject-stratification.**
Subject/helper variance ratio = 21.73× vs primary/helper = 22.11×.
The "row factor dominates helper factor" finding is robust to whether
the row factor is primary identity or subject identity; helper
variance is ~3% in both cases.

**2. Subject decomposition has higher interaction variance.** SS_inter
rises from 12.9% (primary) to 24.6% (subject). This means subject-helper
combinations have more individuated patterns than primary-helper
combinations — consistent with §6qq's finding that mutual-unrecoverability
varies sharply within a primary's subjects.

**3. Biology is uniquely homogeneous across its subjects.**
high_school_biology (+35.1 pp) ≈ college_biology (+34.6 pp), spread
0.5 pp. Every other primary has subject row-mean spread ≥ 15 pp:
medicine 11.1 to 33.3 (22.2 pp), physics 28.3 to 50.0 (21.7 pp),
math −5.6 to +9.8 (15.4 pp). Biology's primary-level "+34 pp" claim
is *not* a subject-mix artifact.

**4. Math primary's primary-level mean (≈ +5 pp) hides bimodality.**
college_mathematics −5.6 pp (collaboration HURTS) vs elementary_mathematics
+9.8 pp. The §6cc/§6pp "math is weak" finding is driven by
college_mathematics + high_school_mathematics (the §6qq high-unrec
subjects); elementary_mathematics actually shows the strongest math-
primary recovery.

**5. Physics has the largest within-primary subject-row-mean span and
the highest mean.** college_physics +50 pp, high_school_physics +46.7
pp — comparable to biology's 34–35 pp but higher. Conceptual_physics
+34.3, astronomy +28.3 pp. The physics primary's helper deltas average
~+38 pp, second only to biology.

### Reconciliation with §6m, §6qq, §6cc

| Section | Finding | §6rr clarification |
|---|---|---|
| §6m | SS_primary = 83.3%, SS_helper = 3.8%, ratio 22.1× | Replacing primary with subject gives SS_subject = 72.1%, SS_helper = 3.3%, ratio 21.7× — same headline |
| §6qq | Within-primary mutual-unrec spans 20–75% (math) | At cell-mean delta level, within-primary spread is 15–22 pp — large but not larger than between-primary spread |
| §6cc | Hard WHO ratio 67.69× | The hard subset's primary-effect should be retested under subject-stratification (queued as §6ss); §6rr is on full grid |

### Implication for the paper

**The WHO-asymmetry headline is unchanged.** "Primary identity
dominates helper identity" can be restated as "subject identity
dominates helper identity" with virtually no loss. Helper-effect-
is-small (3% of variance) survives both framings. The §6qq subject-mix
caveat is real at the *mutual-unrecoverability* and *cell-delta* level
but does NOT dilute the variance-decomposition headline.

> *The 17×6 subject × helper variance decomposition gives subject/helper
> ratio = 21.73× (vs 22.11× under primary × helper, §6m). Helper
> variance is 3.3% (vs 3.8%); row-factor variance is 72.1% (vs 83.3%).
> Within-primary subject row-mean spread is 15–22 pp for math /
> medicine / physics, and 0.5 pp for biology — biology's primary-level
> recovery advantage is not a subject-mix artifact, while math primary's
> low recovery is concentrated in college_mathematics (−5.6 pp,
> collaboration HURTS) and high_school_mathematics + abstract_algebra.
> The "row factor dominates helper factor" finding is robust to the
> choice of row factor.*

Twenty-three converging row-effect tests now (added §6rr: subject-
stratified WHO ratio is essentially identical to primary-stratified;
the §6qq subject-mix caveat does not dilute the variance-decomposition
headline).

## 6ss. Hard-subset subject-stratified WHO ratio: 56.5× — strengthens, not dilutes, the §6cc hard-primary finding

§6rr showed the full-grid subject/helper ratio (21.7×) is essentially
identical to primary/helper (22.1×) — the WHO-asymmetry survives
switching the row factor from primary (5 levels) to subject (17
levels). This section repeats §6rr but on the hard subset (primary
solo wrong), the regime where §6cc reported the largest primary effect.

Audit follow-up #36 (script: `src/scripts/subject_who_ratio_hard.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/subject_who_ratio_hard.json`).

### Setup

For each (primary, subject), restrict to the hard idxs (solo wrong)
and compute pair_correct mean per helper. The cell mean equals the
W2C rate on hard (since solo accuracy on this subset is 0 by
construction). Build the 17×6 (filtered to n_hard ≥ 4) cell-mean
matrix and run the same two-way ANOVA as §6m / §6rr.

### Variance decomposition comparison

| Decomposition | row factor | row levels | SS_row | SS_helper | SS_inter | row/helper ratio |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| §6m (full grid) | primary | 5 | 83.3% | 3.8% | 12.9% | 22.11× |
| §6rr (full grid) | subject | 17 | 72.1% | 3.3% | 24.6% | 21.73× |
| **§6cc-recomputed (hard)** | primary | 5 | **87.3%** | **1.7%** | 11.0% | **50.34×** |
| **§6ss (hard subset)** | subject | 17 | **80.2%** | **1.4%** | 18.4% | **56.54×** |

**The hard-subset subject/helper ratio (56.54×) is LARGER than the
hard primary/helper ratio (50.34×).** Subject-stratification does
not dilute the §6cc hard finding — it strengthens it. Helper
variance shrinks further on the hard subset (1.4% subject, 1.7%
primary) compared to the full grid (3.3%, 3.8%); SS_subject is 80%
of total, the highest single-factor share in the audit.

### Cohen's f: subject_hard 2.01 vs primary_hard 2.62

| Decomposition | Cohen's f row | Cohen's f helper |
|---|---:|---:|
| §6m (full primary) | 2.24 (huge) | 0.20 (small) |
| §6rr (full subject) | 1.61 (huge) | 0.19 (small) |
| §6cc-recomputed (hard primary) | 2.62 (huge) | 0.13 (small) |
| §6ss (hard subject) | 2.01 (huge) | 0.12 (small) |

Hard subset has the largest Cohen's f for the row factor (2.62 primary,
2.01 subject); helper f stays "small" (0.12–0.13). Effect-size
attenuation across the subject vs primary framing is mild (2.62 →
2.01 hard, 2.24 → 1.61 full); the qualitative regime ("huge row,
small helper") survives.

### Filtered subject row means on hard (W2C rate)

Sorted by primary then row mean:

| Primary | Subject | n_hard | W2C rate (%) |
|---|---|---:|---:|
| math | college_mathematics | 4 | **4.2** |
| math | high_school_mathematics | 12 | **9.7** |
| math | abstract_algebra | 6 | 25.0 |
| math | elementary_mathematics | 10 | 38.3 |
| medicine | anatomy | 6 | 27.8 |
| medicine | medical_genetics | 4 | 29.2 |
| medicine | virology | 5 | 33.3 |
| medicine | clinical_knowledge | 9 | 38.9 |
| medicine | college_medicine | 4 | 50.0 |
| medicine | professional_medicine | 7 | 57.1 |
| **biology** | college_biology | 9 | 57.4 |
| **biology** | high_school_biology | 22 | **66.7** |
| **law** | professional_law | 32 | **18.8** |
| physics | conceptual_physics | 16 | 39.6 |
| physics | college_physics | 7 | 50.0 |
| physics | high_school_physics | 14 | 51.2 |
| physics | astronomy | 7 | 61.9 |

(Excluded n_hard < 4: jurisprudence n=2.)

W2C rate range: **4.2% (college_mathematics) to 66.7% (high_school_biology)** — a 16× spread across subjects on the hard subset.

### Five findings

**1. The hard-subset WHO ratio is robust to subject-stratification —
and slightly stronger.** subject/helper = 56.54× ≥ primary/helper =
50.34× on the hard subset; both ratios are >2× the full-grid ratio
(22×). The hard-question primary effect from §6cc is not a primary-
identity artifact — it survives at the subject level.

**2. Helper variance is essentially zero on the hard subset.** SS_helper
= 1.4% (subject) vs 1.7% (primary), down from 3.3-3.8% on full grid.
Cohen's f helper = 0.12–0.13. This is the strongest "no helper effect"
finding in the audit. Choosing the right helper does not help recover
hard questions in any consistent way.

**3. college_mathematics is the single worst-recovering subject (W2C
4.2%).** Of 24 (4 hard × 6 helpers) cell-trial recoveries on
college_mathematics, only 1 succeeds. high_school_mathematics close
second (9.7%, 7 of 72). Both are math-primary subjects; their joint
n_hard = 16 contributes 16/72 of the math primary's hard pool.

**4. high_school_biology is the single best-recovering subject (W2C
66.7%).** With n_hard = 22, this is the largest robustly-recovered
subject in the study. Combined with college_biology (57.4%, n_hard=9),
biology's row spans only ~10 pp on hard — uniquely homogeneous.

**5. The §6qq subject-level finding generalizes from
mutual-unrecoverability to W2C rate.** On hard, the per-subject W2C
ranges (math 4-38%, medicine 28-57%, biology 57-67%, physics 40-62%)
match the §6qq mutual-unrec ranges (math 20-75%, medicine 0-60%,
biology 14-33%, physics 29-44%) inversely as expected (W2C ≈ 1 −
mutual-unrec when only one helper can recover). The row-factor
strength is consistent across both metrics.

### Reconciliation with §6cc, §6dd, §6rr

| Section | Finding | §6ss clarification |
|---|---|---|
| §6cc | Hard primary/helper ratio 67.69× (cell-mean) | Re-expressed as variance ratio: 50.34× — both formulas show the hard regime amplifies WHO ratio ~3× over full grid |
| §6dd | Hard ANOVA F_primary 31.22 | Subject-stratified equivalent on hard: SS_subject 80.2%; same qualitative regime |
| §6rr | Full-grid subject/helper 21.7× ≈ primary 22.1× | Hard subset preserves and strengthens this: subject 56.5× ≥ primary 50.3× |

### Implication for the paper

The §10 abstract directive can now state, with full subject-mix
disclosure:

> *Whether the row factor is primary identity (5 levels) or subject
> identity (17 levels), the variance-decomposition ratio (row vs
> helper) is essentially preserved: 22.1× / 21.7× on the full grid;
> 50.3× / 56.5× on the hard subset. Helper variance is 3% on full
> grid, 1.5% on hard — two- to three-fold weaker than the full-grid
> baseline. The §6qq within-primary subject heterogeneity (math
> 20-75% mutual-unrec, medicine 0-60%) is real at the cell level
> but does not dilute the variance-decomposition headline at any
> stratification level. The "row factor dominates helper factor"
> finding is robust to (a) the choice of row factor (primary or
> subject), (b) restriction to the hard subset, and (c) replication
> across 23 statistical lenses.*

This is the **strongest single sentence the audit has produced**
defending the WHO-asymmetry headline against Reviewer I-style
subject-mix attacks.

Twenty-four converging row-effect tests now (added §6ss: hard subset
subject/helper ratio 56.5× ≥ primary/helper 50.3×).

## 6tt. Per-subject hard W2C bootstrap CI: 20 of 136 subject-pair tests pass BH-FDR; college_math vs hs_biology is the cleanest comparison

§6ss reported subject row-mean W2C on hard ranging from 4.2% (college
mathematics) to 66.7% (high_school_biology), a 16× point-estimate
spread. This section places formal 95% CIs around the per-subject
W2C rates and tests every (17 choose 2) = 136 pairwise comparison
under multiple-comparison correction.

Audit follow-up #37 (script: `src/scripts/subject_w2c_hard_bootstrap.py`,
output: `results/verified_pair_grid_qwen3_1p7b/subject_w2c_hard_bootstrap.json`).

### Method

For each filtered subject (n_hard ≥ 4) we bootstrap-resample the
hard-question rows with replacement (preserving alignment across the
6 helpers via the deterministic seed=42 idx-based join). For each
iteration, the W2C rate is computed across (n_hard × 6 helpers)
trials. n_iter = 2000.

Pairwise: for each unordered pair of subjects, bootstrap the
difference and report two-tailed p ≈ 2·min(P(>0), P(<0)). Apply
BH-FDR at α=0.05 (controls expected false-discovery rate across
all 136 tests) and Bonferroni-136 (α/136 = 0.0004, very conservative).
The bootstrap precision floor with n_iter=2000 is two-tailed p ≈
2/2001 = 0.001, just above Bonferroni-136 — so 0 pairs survive the
Bonferroni cut by construction.

### Per-subject 95% CI on hard W2C rate

Sorted descending by point estimate:

| Subject | primary | n_hard | point % | 95% CI | P(>0.50) |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| **high_school_biology** | biology | 22 | **66.7** | [50.8, 81.8] | **97.7%** |
| astronomy | physics | 7 | 61.9 | [31.0, 88.1] | 76.0% |
| college_biology | biology | 9 | 57.4 | [25.9, 87.0] | 68.0% |
| professional_medicine | medicine | 7 | 57.1 | [28.6, 81.0] | 69.0% |
| high_school_physics | physics | 14 | 51.2 | [29.8, 71.4] | 52.6% |
| college_medicine | medicine | 4 | 50.0 | [33.3, 70.8] | 36.4% |
| college_physics | physics | 7 | 50.0 | [21.4, 78.6] | 44.6% |
| conceptual_physics | physics | 16 | 39.6 | [20.8, 60.4] | 12.4% |
| clinical_knowledge | medicine | 9 | 38.9 | [16.7, 63.0] | 15.8% |
| elementary_mathematics | math | 10 | 38.3 | [20.0, 58.3] | 11.5% |
| virology | medicine | 5 | 33.3 | [0.0, 73.3] | 21.1% |
| medical_genetics | medicine | 4 | 29.2 | [0.0, 75.0] | 17.5% |
| anatomy | medicine | 6 | 27.8 | [2.8, 55.6] | 4.9% |
| abstract_algebra | math | 6 | 25.0 | [2.8, 52.8] | 3.9% |
| **professional_law** | law | 32 | **18.8** | [10.4, 27.6] | 0.0% |
| **high_school_mathematics** | math | 12 | **9.7** | [0.0, 22.2] | 0.0% |
| **college_mathematics** | math | 4 | **4.2** | [0.0, 12.5] | 0.0% |

The cleanest CI separation:
- high_school_biology CI [50.8, 81.8] vs college_mathematics CI [0.0, 12.5]
  — a 39 pp non-overlap.
- The four math + law subjects (college_math, hs_math, prof_law,
  abstract_algebra) all have CI tops below 50%; the two biology
  subjects both have CI bottoms above 25%.

### Pairwise test summary (136 unordered pairs)

| Correction | # passing | Floor on minimum p |
|---|---:|---|
| Uncorrected α=0.05 | **32** | n/a |
| BH-FDR α=0.05 | **20** | depends on n_pass |
| Holm step-down α=0.05 | (similar to BH on this n) | — |
| Bonferroni-136 α/136=0.0004 | **0** | precision floor 1/2001=0.0005 |

The 20 BH-FDR-significant pairs cluster around math-vs-physics-or-
biology contrasts, and around the biology-vs-law contrast.
